<app-context>
# Codex desktop context
- You are running inside the Codex (desktop) app, which allows some additional features not available in the CLI alone:

### Images/Visuals/Files
- In the app, the model can display images, videos, and audio using standard Markdown image syntax: ![alt](url)
- When an app or connector generates or edits media, prefer native media already displayed inline or a local output file already returned by the tool. For remote images, prefer Markdown image embeds when permitted by the app's URL-safety policy.
- For media that cannot be displayed directly, including remote video and audio, use the app's preview or display tool when available. Provide a Markdown link to a usable result URL only as a last resort if no preview or display tool can show the result.
- Do not download remote media to work around display restrictions.
- When sending or referencing a local image, video, or audio file, always use an absolute filesystem path in the Markdown image tag (e.g., ![alt](/absolute/path.png)); relative paths and plain text will not render the media.
- When a user asks to play an audio file, render it using Markdown image syntax with an absolute path (e.g., ![audio](/absolute/path.mp3)).
- When referencing code or workspace files in responses, always use full absolute file paths instead of relative paths.
- If a user asks about an image, or asks you to create an image, it is often a good idea to show the image to them in your response.
- Return web URLs as Markdown links (e.g., [label](https://example.com)).

### Pull request diff links
When referencing code from a GitHub PR, you can link directly to its diff in the app using:
[label](codex://review?pr=PR_URL&path=FILE_PATH&line=LINE&side=right)
URL-encode PR_URL and the repository-relative FILE_PATH. Use a verified one-based LINE from the current PR diff. Use side=left for the original code or side=right for the updated code. Enterprise links must use the hostname of this task's configured Git remote. Use ordinary file links for workspace code.

### Workspace Dependencies
- For sheets, slides, and documents, use the MCP server's `load_workspace_dependencies` tool (`mcp__codex_app__load_workspace_dependencies`) to find the bundled runtime and libraries.

### Automations
- This app supports recurring automations, reminders, monitors, follow-ups, and thread wakeups. When the user asks to create, view, update, delete, or ask about automations, search for the `automation_update` tool first, then follow its schema instead of writing raw automation directives by hand.
- For heartbeat monitors, preserve the user's notification intent in the saved prompt. Unless the user explicitly asks for periodic status updates, instruct the heartbeat to stay quiet while the monitored state is unchanged or non-actionable and to notify only on a meaningful change, completion, failure, or required user action. Do not add instructions such as "leave a brief status update" on every run.
- When an automation should archive a Codex thread on completion, use `set_thread_archived` instead of emitting raw archive directives.

### Thread Coordination
- Treat the terms "task", "thread", "chat", and "conversation" as synonyms when they clearly refer to conversations in Codex. Use "chat" when referring to conversations in the product. In technical discussions, preserve the terminology used by the code, APIs, logs, and documentation.
- When the user asks to create, fork, inspect, continue, hand off, pin, archive, unarchive, rename, or otherwise manage Codex threads, search for the relevant thread tool first: `create_thread`, `fork_thread`, `list_threads`, `list_archived_threads`, `read_thread`, `wait_threads`, `send_message_to_thread`, `handoff_thread`, `set_thread_archived`, or `set_thread_title`.
- When following another task's progress, prefer compact `wait_threads` snapshots over repeated `read_thread` calls. Use one target for single-task coordination and `timeoutMs: 0` for a compact immediate snapshot. `create_thread` dispatches asynchronously, so explicitly wait for progress. Use one bounded call for 1-8 targets with each target's `hostId` and cursor as `afterCursor`; it wakes on the first target that completes or needs attention, and timeout includes the latest commentary for all targets without waking on every commentary update. An up-to-date cursor suppresses already-delivered final text. Separate waits from one task may run serially. Do not narrate unchanged snapshots, and leave approval or user-input requests for the user.
- Only use `create_thread` when the user explicitly asks to create a new thread. Threads created this way are user-owned: they appear in the sidebar, and the user is expected to follow up with them directly. For subtasks of the current request, use multi-agent tools instead, including when the user explicitly asks for a subagent.
- After a successful `create_thread` call, emit `::created-thread{threadId="..."}` for a created thread or `::created-thread{clientThreadId="..."}` for queued worktree setup on its own line in your final response.

### Worktrees
- Prefer reusing a suitable active worktree. Create another when no existing checkout is available or work needs separate isolation. When creating one, choose a short name describing the work, such as `worktree-lifecycle` or `composer-input`. An existing name does not need to match every subsequent task; do not rename or replace a worktree just because the work changes.
- Use `archive_worktree` when a worktree is no longer needed, rather than after every PR. Use `restore_worktree` only when the user requests it or to recover specific work archived prematurely.
- A worktree is free for new work when no ongoing task or process relies on it and any existing changes have been accounted for. Prepare the appropriate branch and base before starting new work. Preserve work still in progress; completed or abandoned work can be archived with local changes or unpushed commits because archive saves a recoverable Git snapshot of tracked files and non-ignored untracked files. Ignored files are not saved; preserve any needed ignored files before archival. Do not delete files just to make a worktree eligible for archival. Preserve pinned, shared, or in-use worktrees when cleaning up. Keep the chat open when retiring an individual worktree, and never close an open PR merely to clean up attachments. Use the worktree tools for cleanup and recovery instead of shell deletion. Check at these lifecycle transitions, not every turn.
- When a new isolated checkout is needed, discover and use `create_worktree` before shell worktree creation. It attaches a managed worktree on the chat's host without moving the chat. Wait for completed paths, then use the returned workspace directory explicitly and request filesystem permissions if needed. Use manual Git worktree creation only when the tool is unavailable or the user explicitly requests it.

### Sidebar Organization
- Use `list_threads` to inspect pinned, custom, project, and task sidebar sections, and `list_projects` for project details. Use `create_sidebar_section`, `rename_sidebar_section`, `delete_sidebar_section`, `move_thread_to_sidebar_section`, `move_project_to_sidebar_section`, `reorder_sidebar_projects`, or `reorder_sidebar_sections` to organize tasks and projects. Moving an item into the pinned section pins it.

### Inline Code Comments
- Use the ::code-comment{...} directive when you need to attach feedback directly to specific code lines.
- Emit one directive per inline comment; emit none when there are no actionable inline comments.
- Required attributes: title (short label), body (one-paragraph explanation), file (path to the file).
- Optional attributes: start, end (1-based line numbers), priority (0-3).
- file should be an absolute path or include the workspace folder segment so it can be resolved relative to the workspace.
- Keep line ranges tight; end defaults to start.
- Example: ::code-comment{title="[P2] Off-by-one" body="Loop iterates past the end when length is 0." file="/path/to/foo.ts" start=10 end=11 priority=2}

### Inline Artifact Follow-Ups
- Format each artifact follow-up as an unescaped Markdown list item, `- :codex-followup[visible phrase]{prompt="Complete user request"}`; avoid closing brackets in the visible phrase and escape double quotes in the prompt.

### Git
- Branch prefix: `codex/`. Use this prefix by default when creating branches, but follow the user's request if they want a different prefix.
</app-context>

For requests to create or edit a standalone LaTeX document, use the built-in editor by default. Create or edit the .tex source with normal file tools, and open the saved file with open_in_codex unless it is already open or the user requests otherwise. Keep follow-up edits in that same file and editor. Use compile_latex_document after editing and fix source errors within its repair limits. Keep the editor open even when compilation fails; preserve the source and report unverified compilation or unsupported project requirements. Discover these tools if deferred. The native editor requires no LaTeX plugin or local TeX installation; do not install either for it. Ordinary math explanations stay in chat.

# AGENTS.md instructions for C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# Global rules — Joshua

These apply on **every** project on this machine, in every session. Project-level
`AGENTS.md` files add to this; they do not replace it.

Keep this file short. It reloads on every message.

---

## Who

Joshua Fisseha — `contact@aspiringattorneys.com`. Builds **LSAT U** (lsatprep.study) and
the Aspiring Attorneys brand. Teaches the LSAT: ~25 one-to-one sessions a week plus
consultations. Windows 11; a MacBook Pro runs some sessions too, so paths differ between
them — never assume `/Users/joshuaf/...` resolves here.

## Where things live

- **AI OS / second brain:** `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` — the Obsidian vault. `context/`,
  `wiki/`, `students/`, `corpus/`, `decisions/`, `brainstorms/`, `skills/`, `projects/`.
  Open this folder rather than starting with no folder, or memory cannot accumulate.
- **Skill catalog:** `dev/ai-os/skills/Codex` (179) and `skills/codex` (154), with
  `PARITY.md` declaring deliberate single-runtime skills and `INDEX.md` cataloging them.
- **LSAT U app:** repo `vnjkoxpl-42905/lsatu-684d89db` — React + Vite + TS + Tailwind +
  shadcn, Supabase backend, hosted on Lovable.
- **GitHub:** account `vnjkoxpl-42905` (auto-generated name, correct account).

## How to work with him

- **Do the whole task.** Deliver the finished thing, then say plainly what you skipped
  and why. Don't hand back a plan when the work was asked for.
- **Look before asking.** If the answer is on disk or reachable through a connected tool,
  go get it. He has Firecrawl, Google Workspace (`gws`), and `gh` — use them.
- **Say what's actually true.** Distinguish verified from assumed, every time. If a step
  failed or was skipped, lead with that. A confident wrong answer costs more than "I
  don't know yet."
- **Surface your own mistakes immediately**, including scope errors you notice mid-task.
- Absolute dates (`YYYY-MM-DD`), never "yesterday" or "last week".
- Don't re-ask what he already answered. Don't pad with options he didn't request.

- **Dictation fixes.** A message shaped like `word --> word` (any arrow, any spacing), or
  "save X in my vocabulary", is a Superwhisper correction. Invoke the `superwhisper` skill
  and apply it in that turn, whatever else is in progress.

## Writing as Joshua

Anything published under his name — course copy, lessons, emails, posts.

Plain, direct, confident. Short sentences. Teach the reasoning rather than asserting the
conclusion; this is a teaching brand and the reasoning *is* the product. Concrete beats
abstract. Never condescend to the reader.

**Never use:** "delve into", "dive deep", "unlock", "unleash", "harness the power of",
"in today's fast-paced world", "ever-evolving landscape", "it's not just X, it's Y",
"navigate the complexities", "at the end of the day", "elevate", "supercharge",
"game-changer", "seamless", "robust", "I hope this helps!", "Great question!",
"Certainly!". No em-dash pile-ups. No paragraph opening with "Moreover," /
"Furthermore," / "Additionally,". No emoji in professional writing unless asked.

Headers should say something, not label a category — "Why students miss this", not
"Analysis".

## Safety

- **Draft by default; send only when he says "send".** Email, messages, posts are drafted
  and shown to him. When he says "send it" (or equivalent) in chat, send it, then log it in
  `wiki/send-ledger.md`. Never send on your own initiative, and never treat a request to
  draft as a request to send.
- **Never delete** files, emails, or records without explicit confirmation.
- The Google Workspace grant is broad (it includes deleting all Drive files), so the
  restraint has to live in tool permissions, not in wording. A prompt is not a permission
  layer.
- Secrets stay in `.env` and the OS keyring. Never paste a key into a note, a commit, or
  a chat.

## Tools already connected

- **Firecrawl** — CLI + 28 skills. Prefer it over MCP; the hosted MCP server was removed
  on purpose (the key would sit in plaintext in `~/.Codex.json`).
- **Google Workspace** — `gws` CLI, GCP project `ai-os-joshua`. Gmail, Calendar, Drive,
  Docs, Sheets, Slides, Tasks, People.
- **GitHub** — `gh` CLI, authenticated.

## Cost discipline

`AGENTS.md` reloads on every message, so keep this file lean. Prefer CLIs to MCP servers
(an MCP server can cost ~18k tokens per message in tool definitions). `/clear` between
unrelated tasks. Run the `handoff` skill past ~25% context rather than waiting for
auto-compact.

---

point of the file.*

<!-- HAND-OWNED since 2026-07-17. This file is the authority for Codex — edit it directly. On any major change, mirror it into CLAUDE.md so Claude and Codex stay in sync. -->
# Jay 2 — operating instructions for Codex (ai-os)

The canonical root is the repository directory containing this file (`C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` on this Windows machine). Resolve paths from the repository root instead of assuming a saved Mac or Windows absolute path. This is the router: it holds pointers, not knowledge. Read it, then follow the routing map to the right file instead of searching blind. Twin for Claude Code: `CLAUDE.md`.

## Identity and mission

You are **Jay 2**, Joshua's executive assistant. This root is his EA workspace and command center. Joshua is the founder of Aspiring Attorneys and builder of LSAT U (lsatprep.study), a premium LSAT prep platform; he is the product, voice, and content authority. Your job: take operations, admin, drafting, and research off his plate so his time goes to teaching and building. Frame work as decisions and tradeoffs, not basics.

Standing duties, run from here without re-briefing: student operations (transcripts, assignments, follow-ups via `students/`), prospect and consultation correspondence (drafted in his voice, **never auto-sent unless a pre-approved template**), platform actions on lsatprep.study, session-note ingestion from Gmail/Drive, and keeping the wiki ledgers (`tasks`/`hot`/`log`) current so the next session starts oriented.

## Routing map

Paths are relative to the repository root.

| Need | Path |
|---|---|
| **Who Joshua is** (identity, preferences, contact) | `context/about-me.md` |
| **The business** (offers, money, channels, gaps) | `context/work.md` |
| **Current priorities** (the 90-day slate) | `context/priorities.md` |
| **What we decided and why** (locked decisions) | `decisions/log.md` |
| Freshest session state (hot cache) | `wiki/hot.md` |
| Master index · work ledger · dated history | `wiki/index.md` · `wiki/tasks.md` · `wiki/log.md` |
| Product ground truth (pointers to live backends) | `wiki/dashboard.md` |
| LSAT teaching knowledge | `corpus/index.md` |
| A student — profile, transcripts, work, correspondence | `students/<name>/` — start at `students/README.md` |
| Voice for anything a student or prospect reads | `wiki/teaching-voice.md` |
| Was it sent? (every outbound draft/send) | `wiki/send-ledger.md` — append-only; Gmail is authority |
| Who the students are (name, email, sessions, links) | `students/roster.generated.md` — regenerate, never hand-edit |
| Learned facts and preferences (automemory) | native store `~/.claude/projects/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/memory/` + `MEMORY.md` index |
| Skill / agent catalog | `skills/INDEX.md`, `agents/` |
| Systems Jay 2 can reach (registry) | `connections.md` — 7 data domains, mechanism/auth/freshness; read by `/audit` |
| Operator brain (how to think about automation) | `references/3ms-framework.md` — the Three Ms; walked weekly by `/level-up` |
| Automation build backlog (what to build next, ranked) | `references/automation-backlog.md` — corpus-derived gaps + tiers; feeds `/level-up` |
| How-it-works reference notes (methods, not state) | `references/` — also `references/claude-plus-codex.md`, `references/obsidian-command-center.md`, `references/second-brain-levels.md` |
| Web research and Firecrawl agents | `references/firecrawl.md` (guidelines in `references/firecrawl/`) |
| Immutable source evidence · intake queue | `raw/sources/` · `raw/inbox/` — one inbox, one archive |
| Archived web clips, with provenance + hashes | `raw/sources/clips/<slug>/` — clip + `PROVENANCE.md` + `SHA256SUMS` |
| Raw personal-data exports (Google Takeout, Gemini session transcripts) | `raw/data-about-me/` — unprocessed PII, git-ignored; never read wholesale |
| Design/plan thinking, dated (grill-me + interview outputs) | `brainstorms/` — supersedes the retired `aios/` reform folder |
| Session handoffs · chat exports | `handoffs/` — operational records, deliberately OUTSIDE `raw/`; `session-handoff` writes here |
| External teardowns and research, dated | `research/` |
| Web-clipped articles (Obsidian Web Clipper) | `raw/inbox/` — unprocessed only; `ingest` moves them to `raw/sources/clips/` when done |
| Application code | `projects/lsatu/` — own repo, own instructions, own remote |
| Other projects (life-path, …) | `projects/<name>/` — each with its own `CLAUDE.md` + `.claude/` |

## Retrieval order

1. `wiki/hot.md` — current-session narrative and open threads.
2. `wiki/index.md` → the page (teaching knowledge: `corpus/index.md`; a student: their folder). Follow links; there is no semantic search and none is needed at this size.
3. The live system — the final freshness escalation.

**Live systems are authority for operational state; the brain mirrors them:** Cal.com + Google Calendar own sessions scheduled/used, LSAT U Supabase owns homework status/dossier/publish state, Stripe owns payment events, Gmail owns correspondence. On any disagreement, the backend wins. The brain is primary — nothing upstream holds them — for transcripts, teaching notes, judgments, prep briefs, curriculum, product specs, and decisions.

**Do not read the wiki unless the task needs it.** Retrieval costs context every turn. Skip it for: code edits in `projects/lsatu/`; shell/Git/file ops; questions the injected memories already answer; a skill run that names its own inputs; anything mechanical and self-contained. Route to it for: what is X · what did we decide and why · who a student is and where they stand · anything drafted in Joshua's voice for someone else · any decision touching roadmap, pedagogy, or UX.

**Wiki operations (LLM-wiki pattern):** the LLM writes/maintains `wiki/` and `corpus/`; Joshua sources, explores, asks. Raw sources are immutable — read, never modify. *Ingest:* read the source from `raw/`, discuss takeaways, write/update the pages it touches, update `wiki/index.md`, append to `wiki/log.md` (`## [YYYY-MM-DD] ingest | Title`); one source at a time, supervised, unless Joshua says batch. *Query:* index first, then pages; file valuable answers back as pages so explorations compound. *Lint:* `second-brain-lint` for contradictions, stale claims, orphans, missing pages.

## Voice

- **No em-dashes, ever.** Joshua never types them; a stray em-dash reads as AI and costs trust.
- **No AI-slop vocabulary** — never "delve," "tapestry," "testament," "demystify," "boasts," "in the realm of," and their kin.
- **Anything a student or prospect will read** routes through `wiki/teaching-voice.md` and is drafted, never sent, unless it is a pre-approved template.
- **When an outbound message is authorized**, preserve the draft's paragraph breaks, list lines, and intentional spacing. After sending, compare the recipient-facing rendering with the draft and confirm exactly one copy landed. Report any mismatch; never resend automatically.
- **Lead with your recommendation, not a menu.** Be decisive; don't over-hedge.
- **Any prompt Joshua will paste elsewhere** goes inside a fenced code block, never inline prose.

## Tools, keys, and secrets

- **The entire secret model is `.env` + `.gitignore`.** The root `.env` (git-ignored — verify with `git check-ignore .env`) is Joshua's single central store for **everything**: API keys, passwords, and bank/account info (decided 2026-07-17). OAuth client secrets live in `~/.config/gws/`, outside the repo. There is no vault, no Keychain, no encrypted store (`records.sparsebundle` is dead) and none is wanted. Never paste a credential into chat — add it to `.env` directly in the editor.
- **Safety is the tool layer, not prompts:** allow/deny tool lists + scoped API keys. Jay 2 acts under its own credentials and never impersonates Joshua.
- **Connected live systems:** Cal.com, Google Calendar, LSAT U Supabase, Stripe, Gmail. Full registry in `connections.md`.
- **Google access is `gws` CLI only** (`gws gmail|drive|calendar|docs …`, keyring-auth; see `skills/codex/gws-shared/`). Never the Google connector MCPs — the CLI is faster, batchable, and exports Docs to markdown.
- **Government IDs stay outside this OS.** Everything else (student names, emails, schedules, transcripts, notes, payment *links*) is ordinary local working material — classify by content, never by filename.
- **No Git remote**, ever, without first completing the scrub checklist in `wiki/HANDLING.md`. The outer repo never tracks `.env`, `projects/lsatu/` (own repo), runtime logs, or locks. Do not create gitlinks.

## Skills

Everything is a skill — **there are no slash commands** (decided 2026-07-17: one kind, one home, one activation path). The catalog at `skills/` and `agents/` is inert storage; nothing auto-loads. An item runs only once **activated by copy** (not symlink) into `.agents/skills/` (Claude: `.claude/skills/`). Creating one writes to **both** the project and the catalog (`skills/{claude,codex}/`), project-scoped — never auto-global. `claude/` and `codex/` subtrees are never merged; a vendor item is a live symlink + a frozen `_snapshots/` copy. `system/scripts/check-skills.sh` validates the model; `skills/INDEX.md` indexes it.

Active in this root. Run `pickup` only when Joshua explicitly asks for it. Do not run it automatically at session start. Use `wrapup` at the end of substantive sessions:

| Skill | Use it when |
|---|---|
| `pickup` / `wrapup` | `pickup` only on Joshua's explicit request / `wrapup` at session end (wrapup persists to the wiki ledgers) |
| `session-handoff` | mid-work context reset near ~250k, or passing work Codex↔Claude — chat-only, writes nothing |
| `audit` | scoring the OS against the Four Cs (structural health, /100) |
| `level-up` | weekly 3Ms interview to find + ship one automation |
| `consultation-inquiry-drafts`, `-followup-drafts`, `inbound-tutoring-replies` | drafting to prospects/students — always via the teaching voice |
| `guided-drill-assignment` | assigning a student a drill |
| `lsat-u-platform-actions` | doing anything ON lsatprep.study from here (assign, post, message, ingest) |
| `tutorocean-profile-fill` | TutorOcean profile work |
| `prompt-architect-refiner` | refining a prompt before it ships |
| `accommodations-email` | "accommodations email to X" (dictation: "accommodations scale"); pre-approved send, QP form attached, self-logging |

When a skill run exposes a gap, improve the skill before moving on. Runtime-specific catalog differences must be justified in `skills/PARITY.md`; undeclared differences are defects.

## Guardrails

- **95% rule.** Make no changes until you are 95% confident what Joshua wants. Ask until you are.
- **Lovable credits require confirmation.** Never take an action that spends or may spend Lovable credits without Joshua's explicit confirmation immediately before the action. Do not infer approval from a broader request to fix, build, deploy, or continue. Free Lovable controls and direct deployment paths may be used without this credit-spend confirmation.
- **Lovable Cloud deploys through Lovable.** LSAT U currently uses a Lovable Cloud-managed backend. Deploy its Edge Functions through the Lovable agent, not the Supabase CLI, unless the backend has first been migrated to a separately managed Supabase project. Confirm this route with Joshua before starting a Lovable deployment.
- **Root stays thin.** Don't accumulate files at the root; work products go to their homes (wiki, students, corpus, projects). Anything left at root gets filed before the session ends.
- **Delegate bulk work.** Exploration or analysis spanning 3+ files goes to a sub-agent that returns summaries, not file dumps.
- **Shell ergonomics.** Run one command per call — chained `cmd; cmd` and `rm -rf X && clone Y` forms trip permission denials that the split commands would not. Never poll with `sleep`. Use backgrounded execution for anything slow, and raise the timeout explicitly for known-slow ops (large moves, typecheck-gated pushes, full verify runs) rather than eating the default.
- **Search before creating.** One authoritative page per subject — update it, don't grow a parallel truth. `wiki/index.md` catalogs every page and updates on every ingest.
- **Preserve provenance and source hashes.** Never move, delete, or modify a migration source root — copy and hash-verify. Never follow a source symlink outside its registered root.
- **Conflicts stay explicit until adjudicated** — never silently overwrite one source generation with another.
- **Automation writes only generated files or fenced generated sections,** through compare-and-swap.
- **Obey nested instruction files** — they win over broader ones for their subtree. LSATU's recovered `CLAUDE.md`/`AGENTS.md` are inviolate: never regenerate them without Joshua's explicit approval.
- **Memory = native automemory.** "Remember that…" writes one fact per file to the native store; `MEMORY.md` indexes it. This file is the rules; memory is learned facts — never mix them. Transcripts and raw captures never auto-promote.
- **Append meaningful system changes** to `wiki/log.md`; log locked decisions to `decisions/log.md`.

## Dual-runtime (Codex + Claude)

- Begin at the Git-root `AGENTS.md` and follow directory-scoped instruction files toward the working path; the nearest file wins for its subtree.
- Keep root instructions inside the runtime byte budget. Move narrow rules to the narrowest owning directory rather than growing this file.
- Activated skills are **copies** in `.agents/skills/` (from `skills/codex/`); agent definitions convert to `.codex/agents/*.toml` with the instructions in `developer_instructions`.
- Claude reads `CLAUDE.md` and never this file. Anything that must hold for both runtimes goes in BOTH files — when you change it here, mirror it there; keep the routing map and guardrails byte-identical between them.
- Activation is unequal by design: Codex reads copies in `.agents/skills/`, Claude reads copies in `.claude/skills/`. Codex runs the catalog minus the wiki/Obsidian and `graphify` skills, so the two skill tables legitimately differ.
- Never have Claude and Codex editing the same file at once; pass work between them by session handoff (summarize → clear → paste). At ~250k tokens, hand off rather than riding autocompact.

## developer

<multi_agent_role>You are an agent in a team of agents collaborating to complete a task.

You can spawn sub-agents to handle subtasks, and those sub-agents can spawn their own sub-agents. All agents in the team, including the agents that you can assign tasks to, are equally intelligent and capable, and have access to the same set of tools.

You can use `spawn_agent` to create a new agent, `followup_task` to give an existing agent a new task and trigger a turn, and `send_message` to pass a message to a running agent.
`send_message` calls may be read by a human, so ensure they are legible. Always put proper spaces between words and/or numbers.
Child agents can also spawn their own sub-agents.

When you provide a response in the final channel, that content is immediately delivered back to your parent agent.
In addition, your final answer may be read by a human, so ensure it is legible.

You will receive messages in the analysis channel in the form:
```
Message Type: NEW_TASK | MESSAGE | FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: <recipient>
Sender: <author>
Payload:
<payload text>
```
You may also see them addressed as to=/root/..., which indicates your identity is /root/...

Note that collaboration tools cannot be called from inside `functions.exec`. Call `spawn_agent`, `send_message`, `followup_task`, `wait_agent`, `interrupt_agent`, and `list_agents` only as direct tool calls using the recipient shown in their tool definitions, such as `to=functions.collaboration.spawn_agent`, since they are intentionally absent from the `functions.exec` `tools.*` namespace. Available tools in `functions.exec` are explicitly described with a `tools` namespace in the developer message.

All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.
- All agents use the same current working directory.
- As a result, edits made by one agent are immediately visible to all other agents.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. Only set `model` or `reasoning_effort` when explicitly requested by the user, applicable `AGENTS.md` instructions, or skill instructions; when doing so, set `fork_turns` to `"none"` or a positive integer string.</multi_agent_role>

{'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"session_id":40670,"r":{"chunk_id":"862e2c","wall_time_seconds":0.0000441,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1131,"output":"---\\r\\nname: lsat-lab-sync\\r\\ndescription: Pull LSAT Lab\'s data in bulk and audit or sync the LSAT U LR question bank and database against it, with scripts instead of clicking through pages. Use when Joshua says \\"pull from LSAT Lab\\", \\"sync with LSAT Lab\\", \\"tag these questions\\", \\"check the bank against LSAT Lab\\", \\"are any answer keys wrong\\", \\"is anything misfiled\\", \\"which explanations does LSAT Lab have that we don\'t\\", \\"full scan\\", or after any import into public/data/PT*-LR.json or the lr_* tables. Covers difficulty, reasoning type, features, answer keys, question text, choice text, missing slots and explanation coverage.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# LSAT Lab sync\\r\\n\\r\\nEverything lives in `system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/` in the vault; its `README.md` has the endpoint details. Run the LSAT U steps from an LSAT U checkout on its own branch (never the main checkout another session may be using), and ship bank changes as a PR.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Two sources, pick by need\\r\\n\\r\\n| Need | Source | Login |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Difficulty, question type, features, reasoning type for all 10,171 LSAT Lab questions | `app.lsatlab.com/my/services/get-questions-just-props.php` + `/api/question-properties`, fetched inside a logged-in tab | Yes |\\r\\n| Full question and answer-choice text, answer key, and written explanations for one question | `app.lsatlab.com/api/questions/<id>`, using `qid` from `data/questions-props.json` | Yes |\\r\\n| Answer key, answer %, trap labels, written explanation, question text, first words of each choice, each test\'s question list | public pages `www.lsatlab.com/lsat-explanations/PT<n>-S<s>-Q<q>` via `crawl_public.py` | No |\\r\\n\\r\\nThe public pages cut choices short. Use the logged-in `/api/questions/<id>` response\'s `answers[]` for full choice text; the official PrepTest PDFs Joshua keeps in Downloads are another source for adjudicating a disagreement.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 1. Refresh the logged-in data (only when tags may have changed)\\r\\n\\r\\nUse a logged-in Codex browser tab, or skip this step and use the existing data/ files. Joshua must already be logged in to `app.lsatlab.com`; never enter his password. Run the two snippets in the README with the browser JavaScript tool: `properties.json` comes back inline; the big question list downloads to Downloads, then move it to `data/questions-props.json`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 2. Crawl the public pages\\r\\n\\r\\n```bash\\r\\npython <vault>/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/crawl_public.py            # every LR slot in the bank\\r\\npython <vault>/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/crawl_public.py --pts 113  # one test\\r\\n```\\r\\n~4,000 pages, 6 workers, resumable (skips slots already in `data/public/`). Run it in the background. Delete `data/public/` to force a refresh.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 3. Pull the database (read-only)\\r\\n\\r\\n```bash\\r\\npython system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/db_pull.py   # from the vault root; writes data/db/\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## 4. Audit\\r\\n\\r\\n```bash\\r\\npython <vault>/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/audit_bank.py --db <vault>/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db --out <report.md>\\r\\n```\\r\\nFindings: `key_mismatch` (bank / database / LSAT Lab disagree), `stimulus_not_on_lsatlab` / `stem_not_on_lsatlab` / `choice_not_on_lsatlab` (wrong or damaged question text), `db_stimulus_not_on_lsatlab`, `in_lsatlab_not_in_bank` / `in_bank_not_in_lsatlab_list` (missing or extra slots), `lsatlab_explained_lsatu_not` (LSAT Lab has a written explanation where LSAT U\'s Coverage State is not Complete), `labels_differ`.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead a sample of every finding type before reporting it. Text checks compare normalized prefixes, so a hit can be a formatting difference; say which you confirmed.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 5. Fix\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Labels:** `tag_bank.py --slots ... | --slots-file ... | --missing`, then `node scripts/build-question-bank.mjs`. LSAT Lab\'s current tags win (Joshua, 2026-09-27). Never touches question type.\\r\\n- **Wrong question in a slot:** the database text is usually right. Confirm on the LSAT Lab page, then rebuild the slot: stimulus + stem + key from the database, choices from the PDF. See `corpus/lr-explanations/fixes/` for the 2026-09-27 repair.\\r\\n- **Answer keys:** a disagreement goes to Joshua before anything changes (\\"I think this question is marked wrong\\"), with the three sources side by side.\\r\\n- **Explanations:** database edits go through a snapshot in `raw/sources/lsatu-lr-snapshots/` first, then Lovable `query_database`.\\r\\n- Ship: PR, merge when Joshua says, then `deploy_project` (free) and confirm the change on lsatprep.study by fetching the file.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Record\\r\\n\\r\\nAppend findings to `corpus/lr-explanations/fixes/index.md` and a line to `wiki/log.md`.\\r\\n"}}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 7.9 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Warning: truncated output (original token count: 10512)\nTotal output lines: 5\n\n{"i":0,"result":{"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"932f2b","wall_time_seconds":6.9447697999999995,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1178,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: teaching-notes\\r\\nsubject: LR explanation model library, 10 to 20 top-tier models per question type\\r\\nupdated: 2026-09-27\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# LR explanation model library\\r\\n\\r\\n334 existing explanations from the LSAT U database, picked as models for writing the missing ones. The [[../golden-set|golden set]] (51) is the short list of the very best; this library is the deep bench, 10 to 20 per question type, spread across difficulty and across each type\'s sub-patterns (each famous flaw on Flaw, conditional vs story-style on Parallel, and so on). Most picks are new; golden picks that turned up are marked.\\r\\n\\r\\n**How it was built.** `system/scripts/lr-explanations/score_corpus.py` scored all 3,640 complete explanations (Evaluate depth, a Goal that names the answer, verdict-only notes, explicit tests, examples, stated rules, correct-answer note length, whether the most popular trap got the most attention; penalties for the em-dash register and for mocking the question). The top 25 to 40 per type were then read in full, every choice note checked against the printed answer text, and scored on the golden-set rubric (P prediction, X precision, E eliminations, T transfer, C clarity, L learner value; /12). Reading was split across 9 parallel readers; each file is one reader\'s judgment, so treat 11 vs 12 as a tie.\\r\\n\\r\\n**How to use it.** When writing a missing explanation, find its type here, read the 2 or 3 picks that share its sub-pattern, and follow the \\"What the best ones do\\" section for that type. `system/scripts/lr-explanations/neighbors.py` finds the closest picks by features and stimulus shape; `show.py SLOT` prints one in full. Joshua\'s cram sheet and LR guideline PDFs (paths in [[../golden-set]]) name the moves and traps.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Type | Models | File | Notes |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Flaw | 20 | [[Flaw]] | Leans on flaws the golden set lacks: ad hominem, sampling, anti-causal, percent vs number, aggregate vs subgroup, best vs only, false choice |\\r\\n| Must Be True / Most Supported | 20 | [[MBT-MBF]] | Conditional chains, Most + conditional, weighted averages, pivots, logical completion |\\r\\n| Must Be False | 17 | [[MBT-MBF]] | Only 8 are truly top-tier; the type has 42 complete explanations in total |\\r\\n| Necessary Assumption | 20 | [[NA]] | EXCEPT is thin: one usable model (PT103-S2-Q22, 8/12) |\\r\\n| Sufficient Assumption | 20 | [[Strengthen-SA]] | Includes PT129-S2-Q7, a clean Refute-a-Conditional model the golden set lacked |\\r\\n| Strengthen | 20 | [[Strengthen-SA]] | Plausibility boosters, ruled-out alternatives, plans, EXCEPT |\\r\\n| Weaken | 20 | [[Weaken-Evaluate]] | Third factor, reverse cause, base rate, analogies, term shifts, EXCEPT |\\r\\n| Evaluate | 17 | [[Weaken-Evaluate]] | About 12 are true models; the type has 25 complete explanations |\\r\\n| Paradox | 20 | [[Paradox-Method]] | Difference-makers, math, plan-didn\'t-work, EXCEPT |\\r\\n| Method | 20 | [[Paradox-Method]] | Includes three Method on Plans / Recommendations models the golden set lacked |\\r\\n| Principle-Strengthen | 20 | [[Principle]] | Principles in answers and in the stimulus, weighing tradeoffs |\\r\\n| Principle-Conform | 20 | [[Principle]] | Stimulus principles as checklists, implicit principles as slogans |\\r\\n| Main Conclusion | 20 | [[MC-Role-Agree]] | Only 5 are difficulty 1-2; more easy models would need a second search |\\r\\n| Role | 20 | [[MC-Role-Agree]] | Concessions, opposing views, intermediate-conclusion chains |\\r\\n| Agree / Disagree | 20 | [[MC-Role-Agree]] | Explicit and assumption-level clashes, EXCEPT, Agree stems |\\r\\n| Parallel | 20 | [[Parallel]] | No difficulty-1 pick besides the golden PT154-S1-Q10 |\\r\\n| Parallel Flaw | 20 | [[Parallel]] | Each famous flaw; no clean Unproven vs Proven False model |\\r\\n\\r\\n**Do not copy from a pick without reading its Weakness column.** Several picks carry a slip, a political example or a flattened diagram that has to be fixed in the new writing, not reproduced. Every defect found, picked or not, is in [[../fixes/model-library-defects]].\\r\\n\\r\\n**Recurring moves across all types** (each file has the type-specific version):\\r\\n- The Evaluate step argues with the argument before the choices (\\"Given that ... how could we argue ...\\"), with worked numbers or one everyday analogy.\\r\\n- The Goal names the answer\'s content, often in two acceptable shapes (formal and conversational, or linking and defender).\\r\\n- Each choice note runs the test its type or wording calls for, and says so in the first line.\\r\\n- The most popular wrong answer gets the longest note, and it says why it tempts before why it fails.\\r\\n- Wrong answers are often rewritten into the version that would have been right.\\r\\n- Recurring rules are stated as rules, so they transfer to the next question.\\r\\n"}}}\n{"i":1,"result":{"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"8d4f7e","wall_time_seconds":7.0685662,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":5681,"output":"Warning: truncated output (original token count: 5681)\\nTotal output lines: 73\\n\\n# Flaw: model picks\\r\\n\\r\\nRead in full: all 40 shortlist candidates (shortlist-Flaw.md), plus golden-set-observations sections 3, 4, 6 and golden-set.md. Only one candidate was already golden: PT151-S2-Q3. I did not look beyond the shortlist, because it had more than 10 that deserve to be models.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Picks: 20** (easy 6, medium 6, hard 8). Rubric order is P X E T C L, each scored 0 to 2, total out of 12.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe existing golden Flaw picks already cover some sub-patterns: slippery slope with abstract answers (PT151-S2-Q3), alternate explanation (PT102-S2-Q9), relative vs absolute (PT139-S4-Q18), an untriggered conditional (PT143-S3-Q18), opinion vs fact (PT118-S3-Q13) and a two-way Nec/Suff (PT142-S4-Q22). So these picks lean toward the flaws the golden set lacks: ad hominem, sampling, anti-causal, percent vs number, aggregate vs subgroup, unproven vs proven false, possible vs actual, best vs only, false choice, and the illegal-negation and illegal-reversal forms at low difficulty.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 1. The picks\\r\\n\\r\\n| # | Slot | Diff | Sub-pattern / features | Rubric /12 | What makes it a model | Weakness | Already golden? |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| 1 | PT134-S3-Q6 | 1 | Correlation to causation, third factor (common cause); \\"ignores the possibility\\" answers | 11 (P2 X2 E2 T2 C1 L2) | The Goal predicts both shapes: the formal \\"correlation is not causation\\" answer, and a named alternate explanation (reverse cause: dead fish feed algae; third factor: factory pollution). On causal arguments the two shapes are \\"infers causation from correlation\\" or \\"fails to consider [reverse cause / third factor]\\" (PT134-S3-Q6).\\r\\n- **Each choice note runs the test its prefix calls for, and says so in the first line.** \\"Fails to consider / overlooks X\\" means ask whether X weakens. \\"Presumes / takes for granted X\\" means negate it and ask whether that weakens. \\"Concludes X on the grounds of Y\\" or \\"mistakes X for Y\\" means X must match the conclusion and Y the evidence (PT155-S1-Q20, PT148-S1-Q2). The strongest version shows that a choice\'s content can be right while its prefix makes it wrong (PT113-S2-Q3 A).\\r\\n- **Do the arithmetic with small round numbers, in a table.** PT148-S1-Q2 uses three tables (100, 200, 120 sold). PT144-S3-Q15 uses five groups of 20. PT101-S3-Q25 uses \\"$50 trillion\\". PT121-S4-Q25 turns 75% of 60 problems into 45 and 15. Numbers are what make the abstract answers on math flaws legible.\\r\\n- **One everyday analogy that has the same structure, placed where the student needs it.** Lottery winner (reversal, PT151-S4-Q23), contact lenses (anti-causal, PT141-S4-Q3), Timmy and the matches (ad hominem, PT154-S1-Q12), pencil as letter-opener (could be vs is, PT107-S4-Q18), traffic past a fender bender (impede vs stop, PT120-S3-Q23), lentil soup (false choice, PT154-S2-Q12).\\r\\n- **The popular trap gets the longest wrong-answer note, and the note says why it tempts before saying why it fails.** \\"This one is very tempting. That first verb is perfect\\" (PT154-S2-Q12 A, 22%). \\"Pause after the first part of the answer\\" (PT120-S3-Q23 E, 23%). \\"This answer is itself making a Necessary vs. Sufficient error\\" (PT154-S1-Q9 A, 24%). Supposing something for the sake of argument is not a contradiction (PT110-S2-Q11 B, 26%).\\r\\n- **Rewrite a wrong answer into the version that would have been right.** PT154-S1-Q9 C (\\"small populations or no populations of gnats ... in climates that are wet\\"), PT120-S4-Q23 D (\\"the workers most likely to be fired are way more productive\\"), PT150-S2-Q4 C (\\"suited to different audiences\\"). This shows the student exactly which word broke the answer.\\r\\n- **State the recurring rules plainly, as rules.** Answers asking for a number, name or definition are never the flaw (PT148-S1-Q2 B, PT107-S4-Q18 C). \\"A factor\\" is not \\"the only factor\\"; \\"reduce\\" vs \\"eliminate\\" (PT128-S3-Q14 C, PT120-S3-Q8 B). Circular, Equivocation and Self-Contradiction show up often and are almost never right. Rebuttal arguments point to ad hominem or unproven vs proven false (PT154-S1-Q12, PT155-S1-Q20). There are two anti-causal objections: \\"even worse without it\\" and \\"groups not fair to compare\\" (PT141-S4-Q3).\\r\\n- **Check each step on multi-step arguments.** Say which inference is sound and which is not, before predicting (PT154-S1-Q9 \\"This mini-argument seems airtight\\"; PT101-S3-Q25; PT110-S2-Q11 \\"we can really only complain about the move ... to her intermediate conclusion\\").\\r\\n- **A conditional answer gets diagrammed, contrapositive included.** \\"Correct answers are usually written in a contraposed form of how we would recognize the argument\\" (PT154-S1-Q9 B). \\"A conditional and its contrapositive have the same meaning, just like \'X is bigger than Y\' has the same meaning as \'Y is smaller than X\'\\" (PT113-S2-Q3 D).\\r\\n\\r\\n## 3. without, until (negate what goes on Left)BICONDITIONAL: if and only if, then and only thenNEITHER/NOR: “not this and not that”NESTED UNLESS: “If then A, then B, unless C”= A + ~C –> B\\r\\nFamous FlawsNecessary vs. Sufficient: reverses or negates a conditionalPart vs. Whole: assumes a trait/property applies to bothCausal: too sure of one possible explanation for somethingUnproven vs. Proven False: no ev / bad arg for X\\r\\n≠ X falseSampling: sample is biased / too-small / unrepresentativeAd Hominem: dismiss view bc source is biased / hypocriticalInappropriate Appeals: emotion / opinion / dubious expertFalse Choice: treats 2 like the only 2 / assumes exclusivityIntent vs. Result: acts like all consequences are intendedEquivocation: a word or concept is used two very diff waysCircular: premise restates or assumes the conclusionSelf Contradiction: two of author’s claims butt heads\\r\\nQuantity TermsAt least one is = some, can, may, might, possible, couldAt least one isn’t = not all, not required, not sufficientAt least a handful = many, often, commonlyMore than 50% = most, typically, usually, generally, tendsto, likely, probably, few fail to be, almost all, nearly all\\r\\nNegative Translations: No A’s are B = All A’s are ~BFew A’s are B = Most A’s are ~BNot all A’s are B’s = Some A’s are ~B’s\\r\\nInferences with “Most” StatementsMost A’s are B Most A’s are B Most A’s are B+ Most A’s are C + Most B’s are ~A + Most B’s are C\\r\\nSome B’s are C More B’s than A’s No inference\\r\\n***ASSUMPTION / OBJECTION FAMILY***\\r\\nIDing Assumptions: Conc has a new idea the Evid didn’t\\r\\nIDing Objections: Given the Evid is true, how could I stillargue the Conc is wrong? (how can I argue the Anti-Conc?)\\r\\nExplain Curious Fact: (common on Str / Wkn / Flaw / NA)Evidence describes a curious comparison, often a CorrelationConclusion proposes a Causal Explanation- Think: Are there any Alternate Explanations?Reverse Causality? Both Effects of the same Cause?\\r\\n- How Plausible is the Author\'s Explanation?Cause w/o Eff? Eff w/o Cause? No Cause, No Eff (control group)?\\r\\nFlaw (Very Predictable. 40% test a Famous Flaw)If no Famous Flaw, ID an Assumption or an Objection- 3 types of answers: Assumption, Objection, DescriptionASSUMPTION: takes for granted, presumes, fails to establish- beware: strong/broad, use negation test (see below) if unsure\\r\\nOBJECTION: fails to consider, neglects/overlooks possibility- if the overlooked thing was true, would it weaken the argument?\\r\\nDESCRIPTION: everything else- Did the arg really do this? If so, is that really a problem?- Infers from (evid) that (conc); Treats (evid) as though (conc)\\r\\nNecessary Assumption (Less Predictable, esp Q15-26)½ test a Missing Link Assumption, ½ Rule Out an Objection- Don’t see a Missing Link? Use Anti-Conclusion to ID an Obj.- BEWARE strong/broad; EMBRACE weak, ruling-out, “not”- NEGATION TEST: which answer, if negated, most weakens\\r\\nSufficient Assumption (answer + evid\\r\\n→ conclusion)- “New Idea” in Conc connected to 1-mention Ev Idea- Correct answer often contrapositive (CP) of prediction- Wrong answers: right ideas, wrong order; lack new idea\\r\\nPrinciple-Strengthen (Very Predictable, Missing Link)- “If [facts from Evid], then [Judgment from Conc]” or CP.- Wrong Answers: different judgment, connect the judgmentto unknowns/opposites instead of the Evid facts, assign thejudgment to the wrong party (cops should vs juries should)or go in the wrong order (If judgment, then facts)\\r\\nStrengthen / Weaken / Evaluate (Less Predictable)- mainly Explain Curious Fact, Plans/Recommendations, orarguments by Comparison/Analogy.STR: Support Plausibility, Neutralize Objections, Add HelpWKN: Attack Plausibility/Assumptions, Raise ObjectionsEVAL: which question has the power to Str or Wkn?\\r\\n***MATCH FAMILY***\\r\\nParallel / Parallel Flaw (Predictable Structure, not Content)- If there are Conditional or Quantified claims, Diagram- If not, try to describe the logic abstractly (e.g. “rules outone explanation in order to prove another”)- Par Flaw: Name Flaw 1st! often Nec vs. Suff / Part vs. Whole- Correct: often scrambles order. Trap: similar topic / words\\r\\nMain Conclusion (Very Predictable)- 90% of them: First line or Pivot/Rebuttal (but/yet/however)- Traps: Last Claim, Intermediate Conclusion, Inference\\r\\nRole (Predict: Main Conc, Supports, Opposes, Neutral)- Like Main Conc, mostly “upside down args” (Conc above Ev)- Intermediate conclusions give support and have support.\\r\\nMethod (common moves: analogies/distinctions, faultyimplications, or eliminating/suggesting alternatives)\\r\\nPrinciple-Conform (Predictable Structure, not Content)Principle/s in the Stimulus: match like a Parallel Q.Principle in Answers: play like Principle Strengthen Q.\\r\\n***INFERENCE FAMILY***\\r\\nMust Be True / Most Supported (Very Predictable)- Find 2+ claims that can be combined. Look for OverlappingConcepts, conditional/causal wording, or pivots creatingtension. MBT favors Conditional and Mathy stuff. MSSfavors Causality and Comparisons.- BEWARE new ideas, EMBRACE weak/familiar ideas\\r\\nMust Be False (rare, Predictable Structure)Answer contradicts a provided conditional or an inference\\r\\nAgree / Disagree (Very Predictable)- Read both, then TAKE PERSON 1’s CLAIMS one at a time:“did person 2 (dis)agree with this claim or not address it?”- If you can’t find explicit overlap, consider assumptions.- BEWARE weak answer language. It’s hard to disagree with!\\r\\nParadox (Less Predictable, esp Q15-26)GIVEN the background fact(s), HOW CAN WE EXPLAIN thesurprising claim?- Usually Comparative: right answer is a helpful Difference- Traps contradict background facts or deepen the paradox.- BEWARE weak answers. EMBRACE strong/new ideas.\\r\\n"}}}\n{"i":4,"result":{"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"d58c6f","wall_time_seconds":5.6136786,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":317,"output":"<class \'list\'> 25\\r\\n{\'practiceTest\': \'PT109\', \'sectionNumber\': 3, \'questionNumber\': 5, \'questionType\': \'Flaw\', \'reasoningType\': [\'Comparative Reasoning\'], \'questionDifficulty\': 3, \'stimulus\': \'The radiation absorbed by someone during an ordinary commercial airline flight is no more dangerous than that received during an ordinary dental X-ray. Since a dental X-ray does negligible harm to a person, we can conclude that the radiation absorbed by members of commercial airline flight crews will also do them negligible harm.\', \'questionStem\': \'A flaw in the argument is its failure to consider that\', \'answerChoices\': {\'A\': \'there may be many forms of dangerous radiation other than X-rays and the kinds of radiation absorbed by members of commercial airline flight crews\', \'B\': \'receiving a dental X-ray may mitigate other health risks, whereas flying does not\', \'C\': \'exposure to X-rays of higher intensity than dental X-rays may be harmful\', \'D\': \'the longer and the more often one is exposed to radiation, the more radiation one absorbs and the more seriously one is harmed\', \'E\': \'flying at high altitude involves risks in addition to exposure to minor radiation\'}, \'correctAnswer\': \'D\', \'questionFeatures\': [\'Argue the Anti-Conclusion\', \'Assumes Similarity\']}\\r\\n"}}}'}]

Since a dental X-ray does negligible harm to a person, we can conclude that the radiation absorbed by members of commercial airline flight crews will also do them negligible harm.\\r\\nA flaw in the argument is its failure to consider that\\r\\n\\r\\nA : there may be many forms of dangerous radiation other than X-rays and the kinds of radiation absorbed by members of \\r\\n    commercial airline flight crews\\r\\nB : receiving a dental X-ray may mitigate other health risks, whereas flying does not\\r\\nC : exposure to X-rays of higher intensity than dental X-rays may be harmful\\r\\nD : the longer and the more often one is exposed to radiation, the more radiation one absorbs and the more seriously \\r\\n    one is harmed\\r\\nE : flying at high altitude involves risks in addition to exposure to minor radiation\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT110-S3-Q4 Unexplained E/E\\r\\nA group of 1,000 students was randomly selected from three high schools in a medium-sized city and asked the question, “Do you plan to finish your high school education?” More than 89 percent answered “Yes. ” This shows that the overwhelming majority of students want to finish high school, and that if the national dropout rate among high school students is high, it cannot be due to a lack of desire on the part of the students.\\r\\nThe reasoning of the argument above is questionable because the argument\\r\\n\\r\\nA : fails to justify its presumption that 89 percent is an overwhelming majority\\r\\nB : attempts to draw two conflicting conclusions from the results of one survey\\r\\nC : overlooks the possibility that there may in fact not be a high dropout rate among high school students\\r\\nD : contradicts itself by admitting that there may be a high dropout rate among students while claiming that most \\r\\n    students want to finish high school\\r\\nE : treats high school students from a particular medium-sized city as if they are representative of high school \\r\\n    students nationwide\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT111-S3-Q13 Unexplained D/D\\r\\nPresident of the Regional Chamber of Commerce: We are all aware of the painful fact that almost no new businesses have moved into our region or started up here over the last ten years. But the Planning Board is obviously guilty of a gross exaggeration in its recent estimate that businesses are leaving the region at the rate of about four a week. After all, there were never more than about one thousand businesses in the region, so if they were really leaving at such a rate, they would all have been gone long ago.\\r\\nThe argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the ground that it\\r\\n\\r\\nA : focuses on what is going out of a system while ignoring the issue of what is coming into the system\\r\\nB : confuses a claim about a rate of change within a system with a claim about the absolute size of the system\\r\\nC : argues against a position simply by showing that the position serves the interest of the Planning Board\\r\\nD : treats a claim about what is currently the case as if it were a claim about what has been the case for an extended \\r\\n    period\\r\\nE : attacks what was offered as an estimate on the ground that it is not precise\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT111-S3-Q25 Unexplained D/D\\r\\nPhysician: Heart disease generally affects men at an earlier age than it does women, who tend to experience heart disease after menopause. Both sexes have the hormones estrogen and testosterone, but when they are relatively young, men have ten times as much testosterone as women, and women abruptly lose estrogen after menopause. We can conclude, then, that testosterone tends to promote, and estrogen tends to inhibit, heart disease.\\r\\nThe physician’s argument is questionable because it presumes which one of the following without providing sufficient justification?\\r\\n\\r\\nA : Hormones are the primary factors that account for the differences in age-related heart disease risks between women \\r\\n    and men.\\r\\nB : Estrogen and testosterone are the only hormones that promote or inhibit heart disease.\\r\\nC : Men with high testosterone levels have a greater risk for heart disease than do postmenopausal women.\\r\\nD : Because hormone levels are correlated with heart disease they influence heart disease.\\r\\nE : Hormone levels do not vary from person to person, especially among those of the same age and gender.\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT111-S4-Q8 Unexplained E/E\\r\\nIt is widely believed that by age 80, perception and memory are each significantly reduced from their functioning levels at age 30. However, a recent study showed no difference in the abilities of 80-year-olds and 30-year-olds to play a card game devised to test perception and memory. Therefore, the belief that perception and memory are significantly reduced by age 80 is false.\\r\\nThe reasoning above is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that it fails to consider the possibility that\\r\\n\\r\\nA : the study’s card game does not test cognitive abilities other than perception and memory\\r\\nB : card games are among the most difficult cognitive tasks one can attempt to perform\\r\\nC : perception and memory are interrelated in ways of which we are not currently aware\\r\\nD : the belief that 80-year-olds’ perception and memory are reduced results from prejudice against senior citizens\\r\\nE : playing the study’s card game perfectly requires fairly low levels of perception and memory\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 5.8 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"1c53f4","wall_time_seconds":5.405526,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1039,"output":"import json,glob,collections,re,sys\\r\\nsys.stdout.reconfigure(encoding=\'utf-8\')\\r\\nDB=\'C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/\'\\r\\nBANK=\'C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/lsatu-wt-glossary/public/data/\'\\r\\nL=lambda t: json.load(open(DB+t+\'.json\',encoding=\'utf-8\'))\\r\\nqs=L(\'lr_questions\'); ex=L(\'lr_explanations\'); st=L(\'lr_explanation_steps\'); nt=L(\'lr_explanation_choice_notes\')\\r\\nS=collections.defaultdict(list); N=collections.defaultdict(dict)\\r\\nfor s in st: S[s[\'explanation_id\']].append(s)\\r\\nfor n in nt: N[n[\'explanation_id\']][n[\'letter\']]=n[\'body\']\\r\\nE={}\\r\\nfor e in ex:\\r\\n  if e[\'style\'] in(\'single\',\'standard\'): E[e[\'question_id\']]=e\\r\\nbank={}\\r\\nfor f in glob.glob(BANK+\'PT*-S*-LR.json\'):\\r\\n  for b in json.load(open(f,encoding=\'utf-8\')): bank[f\\"{b[\'practiceTest\']}-S{b[\'sectionNumber\']}-Q{b[\'questionNumber\']}\\"]=b\\r\\ndef fam(t,stem=\'\'):\\r\\n  t=(t or \'\').lower(); s=(stem or \'\').lower()\\r\\n  for k,v in [(\'parallel flaw\',\'ParFlaw\'),(\'parallel flawed\',\'ParFlaw\'),(\'parallel\',\'Parallel\'),(\'flaw\',\'Flaw\'),(\'paradox\',\'Paradox\'),(\'explain\',\'Paradox\'),(\'necessary\',\'NA\'),(\'sufficient\',\'SA\'),(\'method\',\'Method\'),(\'agree\',\'AgreeDis\'),(\'disagree\',\'AgreeDis\'),(\'principle-strengthen\',\'PrinStr\'),(\'principle strengthen\',\'PrinStr\'),(\'justif\',\'PrinStr\'),(\'principle\',\'PrinConf\'),(\'main\',\'MC\'),(\'role\',\'Role\'),(\'weaken\',\'Weaken\'),(\'strengthen\',\'Strengthen\'),(\'evaluate\',\'Evaluate\'),(\'must be false\',\'MBF\'),(\'must\',\'MBT\'),(\'support\',\'MBT\')]:\\r\\n    if k in t: return v\\r\\n  return \'Other\'\\r\\ndef shape(text):\\r\\n  t=(text or \'\').lower(); out=set()\\r\\n  if re.search(r\'\\\\b(if|only if|unless|any|every|all|requires|must)\\\\b\',t): out.add(\'cond\')\\r\\n  if re.search(r\'\\\\b(cause|caused|because of|leads? to|result|effect|due to)\\\\b\',t): out.add(\'causal\')\\r\\n  if re.search(r\'\\\\b(study|studies|survey|researchers|percent|sample)\\\\b\',t): out.add(\'study\')\\r\\n  if re.search(r\'\\\\b(some|most|many|few)\\\\b\',t): out.add(\'quant\')\\r\\n  if re.search(r\'\\\\bshould\\\\b\',t): out.add(\'plan\')\\r\\n  if len(re.findall(r\'^[A-Z][a-z]+:\',text or \'\',re.M))>=2: out.add(\'2spk\')\\r\\n  return out\\r\\ndef quality(eid):\\r\\n  steps=S[eid]; notes=N[eid]\\r\\n  if not steps or len(notes)<5: return None\\r\\n  txt=\' \'.join(s[\'body\'] for s in steps)+\' \'+\' \'.join(notes.values())\\r\\n  words=len(txt.split()); labels=\' \'.join(s[\'label\'].lower() for s in steps)\\r\\n  nw=[len(v.split()) for v in notes.values()]\\r\\n  verdict=sum(1 for w in nw if w<20)\\r\\n  tests=len(re.findall(r\'negat|would (this|it) weaken|trigger|why should we believe|argue|how could|given that|imagine|for example|let\\\\\'s say|say \',txt,re.I))\\r\\n  dash=txt.count(\'\\\\u2014\')/max(words,1)*1000\\r\\n  q=min(words,900)/100 + (\'goal\' in labels or \'predict\' in labels or \'anticipat\' in labels)*2 - verdict*1.5 + min(tests,8)*0.5 - (dash>3)*4\\r\\n  return round(q,1)\\r\\ncand=[]\\r\\nfor q in qs:\\r\\n  slot=f\\"PT{q[\'preptest\']}-S{q[\'section\']}-Q{q[\'question_number\']}\\"\\r\\n  e=E.get(q[\'id\']); b=bank.get(slot)\\r\\n  if not e or not b or b.get(\'status\')==\'removed\' or not b.get(\'stimulus\'): continue\\r\\n  qu=quality(e[\'id\'])\\r\\n  if qu is None: continue\\r\\n  cand.append(dict(slot=slot,fam=fam(q[\'question_type\'] or b.get(\'questionType\'),q[\'prompt\']),feat=set(b.get(\'questionFeatures\') or []),shape=shape(b[\'stimulus\'])|({\'except\'} if \'except\' in (b[\'questionStem\'] or \'\').lower() else set()),diff=b.get(\'questionDifficulty\'),q=qu))\\r\\njson.dump([dict(c,feat=list(c[\'feat\']),shape=list(c[\'shape\'])) for c in cand],open(\'pilot/cand.json\',\'w\'))\\r\\npicks=json.load(open(\'pilot/picks.json\',encoding=\'utf-8\'))\\r\\noverride={\'PT156-S4-Q10\':\'Paradox\',\'PT117-S3-Q8\':\'Method\'}\\r\\nres={}\\r\\nfor p in picks:\\r\\n  f=override.get(p[\'slot\'],p[\'fam\']).replace(\'Parallel Flaw\',\'ParFlaw\')\\r\\n  sh=shape(p[\'stimulus\'])|({\'except\'} if \'except\' in p[\'stem\'].lower() else set())\\r\\n  pf=set(p[\'feat\'])\\r\\n  sc=[]\\r\\n  for c in cand:\\r\\n    if c[\'fam\']!=f or c[\'slot\']==p[\'slot\']: continue\\r\\n    s=len(pf&c[\'feat\'])*2+len(sh&c[\'shape\'])+c[\'q\']/2-abs((c[\'diff\'] or 3)-(p[\'diff\'] or 3))*0.5\\r\\n    sc.append((round(s,1),c[\'slot\'],c[\'q\'],sorted(pf&c[\'feat\']),sorted(sh&c[\'shape\'])))\\r\\n  sc.sort(reverse=True); res[p[\'slot\']]=sc[:4]\\r\\n  print(p[\'slot\'],f,sorted(pf),sorted(sh),\'pool\',len(sc))\\r\\n  for x in sc[:4]: print(\'   \',x)\\r\\njson.dump(res,open(\'pilot/neighbors.json\',\'w\'))\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.1 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"i":0,"result":{"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"464a4a","wall_time_seconds":0.0000234,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1436,"output":"=== PT112-S3-Q25 Unexplained KEY D\\r\\nAnthropologist: All music is based on a few main systems of scale building. Clearly, if the popularity of a musical scale were a result of social conditioning, we would expect, given the diversity of social systems, a diverse mixture of diatonic and nondiatonic scales in the world\'s music. Yet diatonic scales have always dominated the music of most of the world. Therefore, the popularity of diatonic music can be attributed only to innate dispositions of the human mind.\\r\\nThe anthropologist\'s argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that it fails to\\r\\n\\r\\nA : consider the possibility that some people appreciate nondiatonic music more than they do diatonic music\\r\\nB : explain how innate dispositions increase appreciation of nondiatonic music\\r\\nC : explain the existence of diatonic scales as well as the existence of nondiatonic scales\\r\\nD : consider that innate dispositions and social conditioning could jointly affect the popularity of a type of music\\r\\nE : consider whether any appreciation of nondiatonic music is demonstrated by some nonhuman species of animals\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT112-S4-Q8 Unexplained KEY E\\r\\nArchaeologist: The fact that the ancient Egyptians and the Maya both built pyramids is often taken as evidence of a historical link between Old- and New-World civilizations that is earlier than any yet documented. But while these buildings are similar to each other, there are important differences in both design and function. The Egyptian pyramids were exclusively tombs for rulers, whereas the Mayan pyramids were used as temples. This shows conclusively that there was no such link between Old- and New-World civilizations.\\r\\nWhich one of the following most accurately describes a flaw in the archaeologist\'s argument?\\r\\n\\r\\nA : The argument equivocates with respect to the term \\"evidence.\\"\\r\\nB : The argument appeals to emotion rather than to reason.\\r\\nC : The argument assumes the conclusion it is trying to prove.\\r\\nD : The argument incorrectly relies on words whose meanings are vague or imprecise.\\r\\nE : The argument presumes that no other evidence is relevant to the issue at hand.\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT113-S4-Q16 Unexplained KEY C\\r\\nIn determining the authenticity of a painting, connoisseurs claim to be guided by the emotional impact the work has on them. For example, if a painting purportedly by Rembrandt is expressive and emotionally moving in a certain way, then this is supposedly evidence that the work was created by Rembrandt himself, and not by one of his students. But the degree to which an artwork has an emotional impact differs wildly from person to person. So a connoisseur\'s assessment cannot be given credence.\\r\\nThe reasoning in the argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that the argument\\r\\n\\r\\nA : ignores the fact that anybody, not just a connoisseur, can give an assessment of the emotional impact of a painting\\r\\nB : is based on the consideration of the nature of just one painter\'s works, even though the conclusion is about \\r\\n    paintings in general\\r\\nC : neglects the possibility that there may be widespread agreement among connoisseurs about emotional impact even \\r\\n    when the public\'s assessment varies wildly\\r\\nD : presumes, without giving justification, that a painting\'s emotional impact is irrelevant to the determination of \\r\\n    that painting\'s authenticity\\r\\nE : presumes, without offering evidence, that Rembrandt was better at conveying emotions in painting than were other \\r\\n    painters\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT114-S1-Q6 Unexplained KEY E\\r\\nEvery year, new reports appear concerning the health risks posed by certain substances, such as coffee and sugar. One year an article claimed that coffee is dangerous to one\'s health. The next year, another article argued that coffee has some benefits for one\'s health. From these contradictory opinions, we see that experts are useless for guiding one\'s decisions about one\'s health.\\r\\nWhich one of the following most accurately describes a flaw in the argument above?\\r\\n\\r\\nA : The argument takes for granted that coffee is dangerous to one\'s health.\\r\\nB : The argument presumes, without providing warrant, that one always wants expert guidance in making decisions about \\r\\n    one\'s health.\\r\\nC : The argument fails to consider the nature of expert opinion in areas other than health.\\r\\nD : The argument presumes, without providing justification, that because expert opinion is trustworthy in one case, it \\r\\n    must therefore be trustworthy in all cases.\\r\\nE : The argument fails to consider that coffee may be harmful to one\'s health in some respects and beneficial in \\r\\n    others.\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT114-S1-Q8 Unexplained KEY E\\r\\nPundit: The only airline providing service for our town announced that because the service is unprofitable it will discontinue this service next year. Town officials have urged the community to use the airline\'s service more frequently so that the airline will change its decision. There is no reason to comply with their recommendation, however, for just last week these same officials drove to an out-of-town conference instead of flying.\\r\\nThe pundit\'s reasoning is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that it presumes, without providing justification, that\\r\\n\\r\\nA : increasing the number of tickets sold without increasing ticket prices will be sufficient to make continued air \\r\\n    service economically feasible\\r\\nB : suspending service and losing money by continuing service are the airline\'s only options\\r\\nC : the town officials paid for their trip with taxpayers\' money rather than their own money\\r\\nD : ground transportation is usually no less expensive than airplane transportation\\r\\nE : if the town officials did not follow their own advice then that advice is not worth following\\r\\n\\r\\n"}}}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"i":1,"result":{"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"f895bb","wall_time_seconds":0.0000552,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1153,"output":"=== PT114-S2-Q25 Unexplained KEY B\\r\\nIn our solar system only one of the nine planets—Earth— qualifies as fit to sustain life. Nonetheless, using this ratio, and considering the astonishingly large number of planetary systems in the universe, we must conclude that the number of planets fit to sustain some form of life is extremely large.\\r\\nThe argument is questionable because it presumes which one of the following without providing justification?\\r\\n\\r\\nA : If a planet is Earthlike, then life will arise on it.\\r\\nB : Our solar system is similar to many other planetary systems in the universe.\\r\\nC : The conditions necessary for life to begin are well understood.\\r\\nD : Life similar to Earth\'s could evolve under conditions very different from those on Earth.\\r\\nE : Most other planetary systems in the universe have nine planets.\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT114-S4-Q8 Unexplained KEY D\\r\\nPolitician: My opponent says our zoning laws too strongly promote suburban single-family dwellings and should be changed to encourage other forms of housing like apartment buildings. Yet he lives in a house in the country. His lifestyle contradicts his own argument, which should therefore not be taken seriously.\\r\\nThe politician’s reasoning is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that\\r\\n\\r\\nA : its characterization of the opponent’s lifestyle reveals the politician’s own prejudice against constructing \\r\\n    apartment buildings\\r\\nB : it neglects the fact that apartment buildings can be built in the suburbs just as easily as in the center of the \\r\\n    city\\r\\nC : it fails to mention the politician’s own living situation\\r\\nD : its discussion of the opponent’s lifestyle is irrelevant to the merits of the opponent’s argument\\r\\nE : it ignores the possibility that the opponent may have previously lived in an apartment building\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT115-S2-Q14 Unexplained KEY C\\r\\nActivist: Although the environmental bill before the legislature is popular with voters, it will have mainly negative economic consequences if it is passed, especially when we try to lure new businesses to our country. Great leaders have the courage to look beyond popularity to what is sound policy; legislators ought to do the same by not voting for this bill.\\r\\nThe activist\'s argumentation is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that it\\r\\n\\r\\nA : presumes, without providing justification, that most of the legislators are great leaders\\r\\nB : presumes, without providing justification, that a bill is less likely to pass if it is deemed to have negative \\r\\n    economic consequences\\r\\nC : fails to consider whether there are noneconomic reasons for supporting the bill that outweigh the reason given for \\r\\n    rejecting it\\r\\nD : fails to specify whether legislators usually consider economic consequences when a bill is before the legislature\\r\\nE : takes for granted that if a bill is popular, it will not be sound economically\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT115-S4-Q21 Unexplained KEY B\\r\\nIn a small town, every complaint filed about a plumber\'s work was filed against Moore, who has a relatively small business. So there is good evidence that Moore is a poor plumber who cannot be counted on to do a good job.\\r\\nThe argument is questionable because it fails to consider\\r\\n\\r\\nA : the interests of Moore\'s customers who have not filed complaints\\r\\nB : that there may be few if any other plumbers working in Moore\'s town\\r\\nC : whether Moore\'s business is too small to warrant the kind of generalization drawn\\r\\nD : the number of complaints filed in Moore\'s town about tradespeople other than plumbers\\r\\nE : whether each person who was dissatisfied with Moore\'s work filed a complaint against Moore\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT116-S2-Q5 Unexplained KEY D\\r\\nShipping Coordinator: If we send your shipment by air express, it will arrive tomorrow morning. His contributions significantly shaped the development of modern lighting and communication systems. Yet he had only a few months of formal schooling. Therefore, you do not need a formal education to make crucial contributions to technological advancement.Frank: That is definitely not true anymore. Since Edison’s day there have been many new developments in technology; to make crucial contributions today you need much more extensive technical knowledge than was needed then.\\r\\nFrank\'s reasoning in his response to Maria is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that it\\r\\n\\r\\nA : fails to address the possibility that technical knowledge may be acquired without formal education\\r\\nB : does not consider whether there have been improvements in formal education since Edison\'s day\\r\\nC : relies on using the term \\"crucial\\" differently from the way Maria used it\\r\\nD : presumes that no other inventor of Edison\'s time could have been as productive as Edison\\r\\nE : fails to criticize or question any of Maria\'s statements about Edison\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT117-S4-Q9 Unexplained KEY D\\r\\nManager: When Sullivan was passed over for promotion, people said that the deciding factor was his being much older than the competition. But this is clearly not the case. Several recent promotions have been given to people older than Sullivan.\\r\\nThe manager’s argument is most vulnerable to criticism because it fails to consider the possibility that\\r\\n\\r\\nA : Sullivan was well qualified for the promotion\\r\\nB : age is only one of a number of factors that kept Sullivan from being promoted\\r\\nC : people often associate age with experience and good judgment\\r\\nD : the people older than Sullivan who were promoted had no younger competitors\\r\\nE : Sullivan’s employer tries to keep deliberations involving promotion decisions confidential\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT118-S3-Q6 Unexplained KEY C\\r\\nEditorial: The premier’s economic advisor assures her that with the elimination of wasteful spending the goal of reducing taxes while not significantly decreasing government services can be met. But the premier should not listen to this advisor, who in his youth was convicted of embezzlement. Surely his economic advice is as untrustworthy as he is himself, and so the premier should discard any hope of reducing taxes without a significant decrease in government services.\\r\\nWhich one of the following is a questionable argumentative strategy employed in the editorial’s argument?\\r\\n\\r\\nA : rejecting a proposal on the grounds that a particular implementation of the proposal is likely to fail\\r\\nB : trying to win support for a proposal by playing on people’s fears of what could happen otherwise\\r\\nC : criticizing the source of a claim rather than examining the claim itself\\r\\nD : taking lack of evidence for a claim as evidence undermining that claim\\r\\nE : presupposing what it sets out to establish\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT119-S2-Q24 Steps-only KEY D\\r\\nIt is now clear that the ancient Egyptians were the first society to produce alcoholic beverages. It had been thought that the ancient Babylonians were the first; they had mastered the process of fermentation for making wine as early as 1500 B.C. However, archaeologists have discovered an Egyptian cup dating from 2000 B.C. whose sides depict what appears to be an Egyptian brewery, and whose chemical residue reveals that it contained a form of alcoholic beer.\\r\\nThe reasoning above is most vulnerable to criticism on which one of the following grounds?\\r\\n\\r\\nA : It makes a generalization about Egyptian society based on a sample so small that it is likely to be \\r\\n    unrepresentative.\\r\\nB : It uses the term \\"alcoholic beverage\\" in a different sense in the premises than in the conclusion.\\r\\nC : It presumes, without providing justification, that because one society developed a technology before another, the \\r\\n    development in the latter was dependent on the development in the former.\\r\\nD : It ignores the possibility that the first known instance of a kind is not the first instance of that kind.\\r\\nE : It provides no evidence for the claim that the Babylonians produced wine as early as 1500 B.C.\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PT119-S3-Q2 Unexplained KEY C\\r\\nBenson: In order to maintain the quality of life in our city, we need to restrict growth. Each time the city council was justified in deciding not to restrict growth. Since there is nothing new in this idea of restricting growth, I oppose the regulations.\\r\\nWhich one of the following most accurately describes a way in which Willett\'s reasoning is questionable?\\r\\n\\r\\nA : It presumes that growth is necessarily good without offering support for that position.\\r\\nB : It is based on attacking Benson personally rather than responding to Benson\'s reasoning.\\r\\nC : It ignores the possibility that new reasons for restricting growth have arisen in the past five years.\\r\\nD : It fails to take into account the variety of factors that contribute to the quality of life in a city.\\r\\nE : It overlooks the possibility that the city council of ten years ago was poorly qualified to decide on zoning \\r\\n    regulations.\\r\\n\\r\\n"}}}'}]
