[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 8.8 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Warning: truncated output (original token count: 10252)\nTotal output lines: 1\n\n{"chunk_id":"c6686f","wall_time_seconds":8.435429,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":12589,"output":"Warning: truncated output (original token count: 12589)\\nTotal output lines: 411\\n\\n===== PT150-S2-Q4 Flaw key A\\r\\nSTIM: When politicians describe their opponents\' positions, they typically make those positions seem implausible and unattractive. In contrast, scholars try to make opposing positions seem as plausible and attractive as possible. Doing so makes their arguments against those positions more persuasive to their professional colleagues. Politicians should take note: they could persuade more voters with their arguments if they simply followed the scholars in charitably formulating their opponents\' positions.\\r\\nSTEM: The reasoning in the argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that it\\r\\n## Conclusion (should = opinion)\\r\\nPoliticians could persuade more voters with their arguments if they simply followed the scholars in charitably formulating their opponents\' positions.\\r\\n## Evidence\\r\\nWhen scholars try to make an opposing position seem as plausible and attractive as possible (when they formulate it as charitably as possible), it makes their argument more persuasive to their colleagues.\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nSince this argument is based on a Comparison, we try to think of salient differences in order to object that the comparison is a shaky one.\\r\\n  For example, suppose we said, \\"Marathon runners never run a full marathon as a practice run because it\'s too depleting. LSAT students should take note: they could fare better if they simply followed the marathon runners\' example and didn\'t take a full practice test.\\"\\r\\n  We could think of a salient difference: wait a sec, even though taking a full-length LSAT practice is depleting, the recovery time is much faster than for a runner who runs a 26-mile practice run.\\r\\n  So when it comes to politicians and scholars .... can we think about any salient difference for why the advice of \\"portray your opponents\' arguments as persuasively as possible\\" would hold for the latter but not for the former?\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nLook for an answer possibly saying that even though presenting opponents\' arguments charitably makes scholars look good to their colleagues, it still isn\'t a good idea for politicians to do (since it would make their political rivals seem more level-headed and worth listening to, which is usually the opposite of a politician\'s objectives).\\r\\n-- A: When we see Flaw answers start with fails to consider / ignores the possibility, we treat them like Weaken answer choices and ask ourselves whether we could object to the argument using this idea.\\r\\n\\r\\nWould it hurt this argument if we said, \\"Hey, author -- yes, that approach (presenting opponents\' views charitably) works with one kind of audience (fellow scholars), but it will not work well with another (a politician\'s audience)?\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nSure, that sounds like an objection. It\'s essentially just punching back at the author\'s suggestion that was works well for scholars will also work well for politicians.\\r\\n\\r\\nWe can imagine that people at a rally for Bernie Sanders might not respond well if Bernie presents Ted Cruz\'s arguments super charitably, and people at a rally for Ted Cruz might not respond well if Ted presents Bernie\'s arguments very charitably.\\r\\n-- B: When we see Flaw answers start with fails to consider / ignores the possibility, we can ask ourselves whether we could object to the argument using this idea.\\r\\n\\r\\nCould we say, \\"Hey, author -- I know you\'re recommending that politicians express their opponents\' positions very charitably, but it\'s difficult to come up with charitable formulations of opposing positions\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nNot really. The author never promised it would be easy. She only said that, \\"if a politician charitably formulated her opponents\' positions, then she could persuade more voters with her arguments\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nThis answer would be objecting to the If-condition, which is not something we ever do. We can only object to this conclusion by describing a world in which politicians do succeed in charitably formulating their opponents\' positions, but it does not persuade more voters to agree with their arguments.\\r\\n-- C: The author does establish a difference between the politician\'s style of argumentation (make opponent\'s position seem implausible and unattractive) and the scholar\'s style (make opposing position seem as plausible and attractive as possible).\\r\\n\\r\\nThe second half of this answer is complaining that the author \\"focused on different styles even though those two styles might be suited to similar audiences\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nThat complaint doesn\'t make any sense. Are we trying to say that scholars and politicians have similar audiences? That doesn\'t sound like our complaint. It sounds more like the opposite of our complaint. The way a scholar talks to other scholars seems like it could play be vastly different rules than the way a politician talks to voters.\\r\\n\\r\\nSo this answer would be closer to being okay if said, \\"even though those styles might be suited to different audiences\\". That would allow us to complain, \\"Yes, author, they have two very different styles of argumentation -- but that\'s because they have to very different audiences, so it\'s appropriate for the styles to be different\\".\\r\\n-- D: When we see Flaw answers start with takes for granted / presumes, we treat them like Necessary Assumption answer choices and ask ourselves whether the author needed to assume this idea.\\r\\n\\r\\nWas this author assuming that \\"both scholars and politicians have persuasion as their aim\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nWell, we could say, \\"No, the author is only thinking that both scholars and politicians would be more persuasive if they presented opposing positions charitably.\\" An author could conclude, \\"If you wear cologne, you will be more attractive to others\\", and that isn\'t assuming that \\"being more attractive to others is a goal we have. It\'s just speaking to cause and effect.\\r\\n\\r\\nWe could also say, \\"sure, the author is assuming that both scholars and politicians have persuasion as one of their aims.\\" But we still wouldn\'t say that our biggest logical complaint with this author is that she\'s assuming that both scholars and politicians want to persuade. Our problem is that the author is assuming that because a technique has a certain effect for scholars, it will have a similar effect for politicians.\\r\\n-- E: When we see Flaw answers start with takes for granted / presumes, we treat them like Necessary Assumption answer choices and ask ourselves whether the author needed to assume this idea.\\r\\n\\r\\nWas this author assuming that \\"politicians formulate the positions of opponents uncharitably even when they share the same position?\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nNo, the author said that politicians typically make opponents\' positions seem implausible, not that they always do. The author doesn\'t need to assume that politicians frame an opponent\'s position unfairly even when they hold the same position.\\r\\n===== PT147-S1-Q6 Flaw (type inferred) key B\\r\\nSTIM: Some have argued that body size influences mating decisions throughout all societies. Their argument rests largely on self-reports of university-age students and on analyses of personal advertisements in newspapers for dating partners.\\r\\nSTEM: The reasoning in the argument described above is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that the argument\\r\\n## Conclusion\\r\\nBody size influences mating decisions throughout all societies.\\r\\n## Evidence (argument rests on)\\r\\nJust look at the self-reports of college-age students, and look at personal advertisements in newspapers for dating partners.\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nThis argument is presented in a really weird style. We\'re being told what some people have concluded, and we\'re being told what sort of evidence they consulted, but there isn\'t actually any evidentiary claim presented. Like, what were the self-reports of college age students? When they analyzed personal ads for romantic partners, what did they find? We don\'t get any supporting premises; we only hear where the author got his premise, and so we\'re apparently supposed to find Flaw with that itself. It\'s sort of like if we heard, \\"Some have argued that what kids want most for Christmas is a hula hoop. Their argument rests largely on asking people in nursing homes what they thought would be a kid\'s #1 Christmas wish.\\" We\'re supposed to react to the Evidence and say that the flaw is that \\"you\'re evidence is looking at a totally inappropriate source\\". Why would you ask old people what kids want for Christmas? They\'re going to tell you what a kid in the 1950\'s wanted for Christmas, not what a modern kid wants. So similarly, if you\'re trying to figure out whether body size influences mating decisions throughout all societies, then why should we criticize you for asking college students what influences their mating decisions, or for analyzing dating ads in the paper to see what\'s motivating their dating decisions? Well, it\'s an incredibly shallow source of evidence, double entendré intended. It\'s shallow as in way too small a sample. It\'s bad to extrapolate from what is true among college students and personal ads to what is true \\"throughout all societies\\". It\'s also shallow as in not representative of all mating behavior. College students and people placing hook-up ads in the paper might be more focused on looks, whereas if you looked in more \\"wholesome\\" places you\'d find people whose partner\'s body size did not play a meaningful role in their mating decision.\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nLook for an answer reacting to how ludicrously strong the conclusion is (X influences Y throughout all societies) based on the fact that the evidence considered is an incredibly narrow swath of the human condition (some college students\' self-reporting and some personal ads in newspapers).\\r\\n-- A: This answer describes a variation of the famous Causal flaw, in which an author overconfidently concludes one causal explanation for something, without considering other possible explanations.\\r\\nCould we fairly say that the author concludes that \\"one kind of event causes another kind of event\\"?\\r\\nNot really. The conclusion is causal (\'body size influences mating decisions\'), but we can\'t really call \'body size\' an event.\\r\\nAnd we didn\'t find ourselves yelling at this author, \\"Hey, man, maybe both body size and mating decisions are being caused by some 3rd factor.\\"\\r\\n-- B: This answer describes a variation of the famous Sampling flaw, in which an author\'s conclusion is a generalization / extrapolation, and the evidence is a sample that we criticize for being too small, an unrepresentative slice of the population, a self-selecting sample, or at high risk of having dishonest responses.\\r\\nThe sample of \\"college students and personal ads\\" is likely to be unrepresentative of \\"mating decisions throughout all societies\\".\\r\\nAsking the co-eds at Arizona State or the users of Tinder how they make their mating decisions and expecting that their answers will be the same as if we asked a remote hunter-gather society in Ethiopia seems like a stretch.\\r\\nSo the author should not be generalizing about all societies based on this incredibly narrow subset of data points.\\r\\n-- C: Could we fairly say that the author concludes that \\"an effect has only one cause\\"?\\r\\nNo. The conclusion says \'body size influences mating decisions\', which doesn\'t mean it\'s the only cause of mating decisions.\\r\\n-- D: Does the author draw a conclusion about individual persons?\\r\\nNo. The conclusion says \'body size influences mating decisions throughout all societies\'.\\r\\n-- E: It\'s pretty freaky that they would put another answer choice referencing the Sampling flaw.\\r\\nIt does seem like the evidence is too small, given that the conclusion is about all the societies of the world. But can we say it\'s \\"a very small number of individual cases\\"?\\r\\nNot necessarily. We don\'t know how many university-age students were polled. We don\'t know how many personal ads were analyzed. And we\'re only told \\"their argument rests largely on\\" these things, so we also don\'t know what the rest of the evidence consisted of.\\r\\nIf 1,000 students were questioned and 1,000 personal ads were analyzed, we would not call that a very small individual number of cases.\\r\\nBut we could still say that it\'s a very unrepresentative slice of the mating-behavior pie. No matter how accurate a picture we have about the mating decisions of college students and people who place personal ads, we still won\'t feel comfortable assuming that their mating decisions are akin to those of all other people.\\r\\n===== PT154-S1-Q12 Flaw key C\\r\\nSTIM: Art history professor: Costa criticizes my theories about the distinction between baroque and neoclassical Austrian painting. He argues that since there are no features possessed by all and only the works from a given historical period, assigning works of art to period styles is intellectually bankrupt. His reasoning can be discounted, however, since his own current theories on the transition from classical to romantic French opera also presuppose such an assignment.\\r\\nSTEM: Which one of the following most accurately describes a flaw in the art history professor\'s argument?\\r\\n## Conclusion\\r\\nCosta\'s reasoning can be discounted.\\r\\n## Evidence (since)\\r\\nHis own current theories presuppose the same assignment he\'s criticizing me for.\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nWe could get into the details of Costa said, but they are immaterial to recognizing the Famous Flaw that\'s happening here.\\r\\nWhenever we\'re doing Flaw and we see a Rebuttal style argument, we know to look for these two of the 10 Famous Flaws:\\r\\n1. Ad Hominem — in which an author dismisses the view of person X because person X has some biased interest in the matter or because person X has come conflicting past behavior.\\r\\n2. Unproven vs. Proven False — in which an author concludes that X is false, since there is a lack of evidence that X is true or since someone\'s attempt to prove that X is true was a flawed attempt.\\r\\nThe conclusion here isn\'t saying that Costa is wrong, so we\'re not dealing with #2. It\'s saying Costa\'s view can be discounted / dismissed / disregarded, which is more the style of #1.\\r\\nWhy are we discounting Costa\'s critique? Because \\"Costa does the same thing\\". This is a little like getting busted by Mom for playing with matches and trying to argue that we don\'t deserve punishment because \\"Timmy did the same thing!\\"\\r\\nMom will say, \\"Okay, then both you and Timmy did something wrong.\\"\\r\\nSimilarly, we could say to this professor, \\"Both you and Costa deserve to be criticized for trying to assign works of art to period styles.\\"\\r\\nSomeone who commits tax fraud should still be listened to when they accuse person X of committing tax fraud. Someone who had their license suspended for a DUI should still be listened to when they tell person X that X has had too much to drink to get behind the wheel.\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nLook for something saying, \\"Just because Costa is guilty of X doesn\'t mean we should discount Costa when he argues that we are guilty of X\\".\\r\\nThe answer can also say that the author fails to address the substantive point Costa makes, distracting us instead with irrelevant details about what Costa himself does in his theories.\\r\\n-- A: This answer describes the #1 famous flaw Necessary vs. Sufficient, in which the author presents a conditional logic premise and then illegally applies that rule in a backwards or negated fashion.\\r\\n\\r\\nThere\'s no conditional logic premise here. The only premise is \\"Costa\'s own theories presuppose such an assignment\\".\\r\\n-- B: Since this answer starts with fails to consider / overlooks the possibility, we can ask ourselves whether the idea that follows would Weaken.\\r\\n\\r\\nCan we hurt the professor\'s argument by telling her that \\"theoreticians can hold radically different theories at different times\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nNo, that doesn\'t seem to be a relevant objection. Her premise is just about Costa\'s current theories, so who cares what different theories he (or other theoreticians) held at different times.\\r\\n-- C: We could understand this answer to be a \\"conflicting behavior\\" version of the famous Ad Hominem flaw: \\"My dad tells me not to smoke cigarettes because it will mess up my lungs. His reasoning can be discounted, however, since he smokes cigarettes\\". Obviously, Dad could be right about his advice and wrong about his behavior (do as I say, not as I do).\\r\\n\\r\\nWe could otherwise understand this answer by reacting to its structure:\\r\\nthe argument does X merely on the grounds that Y\\r\\n\\r\\nThis means that X should match the conclusion and Y should match the evidence. Was the conclusion \\"rejecting the reasoning on which a criticism is based\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nYes: Costa\'s reasoning should be discounted was our conclusion, and Costa\'s reasoning was being used to criticize the professor\'s theories.\\r\\n\\r\\nDoes the evidence say that \\"that same criticism could be applied to the theories of the person who offered the criticism\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nYes! Costa said that it was intellectually bankrupt to assign works of art to period styles, but the author\'s premise (since) is saying that Costa\'s own theories presuppose an assignment of works of art to period styles.\\r\\n-- D: This answer describes the famous flaw Part vs. Whole, in which the author assumes that a trait that belongs to the Whole is therefore possessed by each Part of the whole (or vice versa).\\r\\n\\r\\nIf an answer says \\"presumes that what is true of X must also be true of Y\\", then we want to see if X matches the evidence and Y matches the conclusion.\\r\\n\\r\\nDid the premise talk about \\"what is true of art in general\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nHeavens, no. The premise says \\"Costa\'s own current theories presuppose the same assignment of works of art to period styles\\". We can stop reading here.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conclusion also does not match; it is not a claim about \\"every particular style of art\\". It\'s the claim that \\"Costa\'s reasoning can be discounted\\".\\r\\n-- E: Since this answer starts with presumes / takes for granted, we can ask ourselves whether the idea that follows would be a Necessary Assumption.\\r\\n\\r\\nWas the author assuming that \\"theories about one type of art cannot be compared to theories about another\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nNope. That\'s a crazy-extreme claim, and our author isn\'t saying anything related to that. She\'s only talking about Costa\'s theories. Also, to the extent that the author is saying that Costa\'s theories are guilty of the same \\"sin\\" that Costa accuses the author\'s theories of, the author seems to think that at least some theories can be compared to each other.\\r\\n===== PT134-S3-Q6 Flaw key D\\r\\nSTIM: Local resident: An overabundance of algae must be harmful to the smaller fish in this pond. During the fifteen or so years that I have lived here, the few times that I have seen large numbers of dead small fish wash ashore in late summer coincide exactly with the times that I have noticed abnormally large amounts of algae in the water.\\r\\nSTEM: The local resident\'s argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that it\\r\\n## Conclusion\\r\\nAn overabundance of algae must be harmful to the smaller fish in this pond.\\r\\n## Evidence\\r\\nDuring the 15 or so years I\'ve lived here, the few times that I\'ve seen large numbers of dead small fish wash ashore in late summer coincide exactly with the times that I have noticed abnormally large amounts of algae in the water.\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nThere aren\'t obvious keywords here indicating Conclusion vs. Evidence, but the 2nd sentence is clearly evidence, because it\'s a historical fact, not an opinion or inference the author is deriving. The first sentence\'s keyword is \\"must\\". That word often shows up in conditional statements (such as \\"In order to do X, you must do Y\\"). But it\'s als…252 tokens truncated…nSTEM: The reasoning in Moore\'s argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that the argument\\r\\n## Conclusion\\r\\nSunscreen lotions do not effectively block skin-cancer-causing UV radiation.\\r\\n## Evidence\\r\\nMany studies show that people who have consistently used these lotions develop, on average, as many skin cancers as those who have rarely, if ever, used them.\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nThis is an *Anti-Causal* argument, because the conclusion is saying \\"X does **not** cause Y\\". The evidence is that people who use sunscreen tend to get as much skin cancer as do people who don\'t. The classic *Anti-Causal* objection is to say, \\"yes but those two groups aren\'t fair to compare\\".\\r\\nConsider this *Anti-Causal* analogy:\\r\\nContact lenses, which are designed to improve people\'s vision, do not do so effectively. Studies show that people who wear contact lenses are able, on average, to see as far down the road as are people who don\'t wear contact lenses.\\r\\nWe would shout, \\"That\'s because the first group of people is wearing their contact lenses! Compare these two groups with no one wearing contact lenses and you\'ll see that the first group can barely focus 10 feet in front of them, while the group of people who doesn\'t wear contacts can see 200 feet in front of them. Since wearing contact lenses allows this first group to *also* see 200 feet in front of them, clearly these contact lenses *are* effectively improving their vision.\\"\\r\\nSimilarly, maybe the people who wear sunscreen are people who really need it (they are super fair-skinned or they spend tons of time out in the open sun) whereas people who don\'t wear it are darker-skinned, indoor types.\\r\\nIf the first group weren\'t wearing sunscreen, then their skin cancer levels would be through the roof. The fact that wearing sunscreen brings their skin cancer numbers down to the same level as those of darker-skinned, indoor types makes it seem like the sunscreen is effectively blocking some cancer-causing radiation.\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nWe need to defend the idea that sunscreen DOES help block our cancerous UV radiation, so we need to \\"make an excuse\\" for the evidence. We need a way to explain why sunscreen users have similar skin cancer rates to non-sunscreen people, such that we can still argue that the sunscreen IS helping the sunscreen users to block out cancerous UV rays.\\r\\n-- A: Since this starts with *takes for granted / presumes*, we ask ourselves whether this idea qualifies as a Necessary Assumption.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt doesn\'t. It\'s way too strong and irrelevant. The author doesn\'t need to assume that **there are no possible** benefits to using sunscreen; if we say that sunscreen at least has the health benefit of moisturizing one\'s face, that wouldn\'t hurt the argument at all.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conclusion is strictly about whether or not sunscreen blocks cancer-causing UV radiation. Anything else it happens to do is out of scope.\\r\\n-- B: This answer is pretty tempting since we might have thought about objecting to the anti-causal conclusion by saying, \\"Sure, people who use sunscreen have a similar rate of **getting** skin cancer, but the **type** of skin cancer is less severe, so therefore the sunscreen is still doing something.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nThis isn\'t terrible, but the conclusion is saying that sunscreen doesn\'t effectively block skin-cancer-causing UV radiation. If someone wears sunscreen and gets skin cancer, even a mild case, then isn\'t it fair to say that their sunscreen did not effectively block skin-cancer-causing UV radiation?\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the verb were \\"does not effectively reduce UV radiation\\" then we could probably point to a mild case of skin cancer and say, \\"This is still a sunscreen success story. The skin cancer would have been even worse without the sunscreen.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nBut because of the definitive verb block, if someone wearing sunscreen gets any form of skin cancer, then it\'s fair to say that the sunscreen didn\'t effectively block the cancer-causing radiation.\\r\\n-- C: Since this starts with *fails to consider / ignores the possibility*, we can ask ourselves whether this idea qualifies as a weakening Objection.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt doesn\'t, because this conclusion is strictly about the effectiveness of sunscreens that are designed to effectively block cancer-causing UV radiation.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe effectiveness of sunscreens that aren\'t designed to block that radiation is totally irrelevant to this conversation.\\r\\n-- D: The evidence in this argument isn\'t about the probability of people in different groups developing cancer (that\'s a future-looking guess).\\r\\n\\r\\nThe evidence is data from scientific studies tabulating the rate at which people did get skin cancer.\\r\\n-- E: Since this starts with *fails to consider / ignores the possibility*, we can ask ourselves whether this idea qualifies as a weakening Objection.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt does, because if the people who use sunscreen spend more time in the sun than people who don\'t, it\'s not fair to compare these two groups. The former group is at a much higher risk for skin cancer (since they get more exposure to the sun).\\r\\n\\r\\nIf none of them wore any sunscreen, then the people exposed to more sun would have higher rates of skin cancer. The fact that wearing sunscreen brings their cancer rates down to the level of people who have less exposure to the sun shows that the sunscreen is working.\\r\\n\\r\\nOn Anti-Causal arguments, we\'re typically lodging one of two (often related) objections:\\r\\n1. It would be even worse/better without X\\r\\n2. These two groups/situations aren\'t fair to compare\\r\\n\\r\\nHere, we\'re sort of doing both. These two groups aren\'t fair to compare because the sunscreen group has a much higher underlying risk of skin cancer due to their greater risk of exposure. Thus, their rate of skin cancer would be even higher if they weren\'t wearing sunscreen.\\r\\n\\r\\nWe can also think of this as offering an Alternate Explanation for the Curious Fact. The Curious Fact was, \\"How come sunscreen users have the same cancer rate as non-sunscreen users?\\", and the author\'s Explanation was, \\"Because sunscreen doesn\'t actually help block cancer causing radiation\\", and here our Alternate Explanation is, \\"no, the reason they have the same rate is because sunscreen users start out at a much higher risk of skin cancer\\".\\r\\n===== PT148-S1-Q2 Flaw key A\\r\\nSTIM: Five years ago, the hair dryer produced by the Wilson Appliance Company accounted for 50 percent of all sales of hair dryers nationwide. Currently, however, Wilson Appliance\'s product makes up only 25 percent of such sales. Because of this decline, and because the average net income that Wilson receives per hair dryer sold has not changed over the last 5 years, the company\'s net income from sales of the product must be only half of what it was 5 years ago.\\r\\nSTEM: The reasoning in the argument is flawed because the argument\\r\\n## Conclusion\\r\\nThe company\'s net income from sales of the product must be only half of what it was 5 years ago.\\r\\n## Evidence (because)\\r\\n  - 5 yrs ago, 50% of all hair dryers purchased nationwide were Wilson\'s.\\r\\n  - Now, only 25% of all hair dryers purchased nationwide are Wilson\'s.\\r\\n  - The average net income that Wilson receives per hair dryer has not changed over the last 5 years.\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nThis is a very math-y argument, so the flaw is probably related to math. When we see arguments involving percentages, they often involve an illicit switch between percentages and numbers.\\r\\n  For example, if an author saw that a certain company went from being 40% female to being 50% female, they would assume that there were more females than before. But just because a percentage went up doesn\'t mean the raw number went up. It could be like this:\\r\\n  ```\\r\\n  THEN       NOW\\r\\n  4 women    4 women\\r\\n  6 men      4 men\\r\\n  40% women  50% women\\r\\n  ```\\r\\n  In this argument, Wilson went from being 50% of hairdryers sold to 25%. Is the author assuming that since the percentage went down, the raw number went down?\\r\\n  Yes! And if the raw number did go down, then the conclusion is totally valid.\\r\\n  If Wilson sold a smaller number of hairdryers last year, compared to five years ago, but they still make the exact same net income (i.e. profit) per hairdryer, then they would be making less net income from that product. Again, let\'s consider numbers.\\r\\n  ```\\r\\n                     5 yrs. ago   Now\\r\\n  sold nationally    100          100\\r\\n  % that are Wilson  50%          25%\\r\\n  # that are Wilson  50           25\\r\\n  ```\\r\\n  If they make $10 of profit off each hairdryer, then five years ago they made $500 and now they made $250.\\r\\n  But let\'s not play the author\'s game. How could a smaller percentage not be a smaller number? If the total number of hairdryers sold nationally has doubled, then Wilson would be selling the same number of hairdryers.\\r\\n  ```\\r\\n                     5 yrs. ago   Now\\r\\n  sold nationally    100          200\\r\\n  % that are Wilson  50%          25%\\r\\n  # that are Wilson  50           50\\r\\n  ```\\r\\n  Now, the author\'s conclusion is wrong. They sold 50 hair dryers in both cases, so their net income from that product hasn\'t changed.\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nIn order for the author\'s conclusion to be valid, we need there to be the same number of hairdryers sold nationally, now vs. 5 yrs ago.\\r\\n  If that number is different nowadays, then Wilson won\'t be selling exactly half as many hairdryers and so their profit from hairdryers won\'t be exactly half of what it was 5 years ago.\\r\\n-- A: Any time a Flaw answer is structured like\\r\\nconfuses X with Y\\r\\nmistakes X for Y\\r\\nwe want to see if X seems to be referring to what the evidence talked about and if Y seems to be referring to what the author concluded/assumed.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe evidence was indeed talking about market share. 5 yrs ago, Wilson had 50% of the national hairdryer market. Nowadays, that share has shrunk to 1/4, to 25% of national hairdryers.\\r\\n\\r\\nBut that doesn\'t mean that there has been any decline in total sales, in the number of hairdryers sold. As we showed before, if the number of hairdryers sold nationally changed, then you can\'t make that \\"1/2 as big a percent = 1/2 as big net income\\" inference.\\r\\n\\r\\nEven a modest increase makes the conclusion wrong.\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\n                     5 yrs. ago   Now\\r\\nsold nationally      100          120\\r\\n% that are Wilson    50%          25%\\r\\n# that are Wilson    50           30\\r\\nnet income           $500         $300\\r\\n($10 / each)\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conclusion said that net income was only half, but here is a possibility where the net income was 3/5 as big (more than 1/2).\\r\\n\\r\\nSince the author goes from establishing that market share got cut in half to concluding that net income got cut in half, he must assume that sales volume got cut in half.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis answer is saying that he\'s conflating what happened to market share with what he assumes also happened to sales volume.\\r\\n-- B: This sort of answer, which asks for specific values / measurements / names / definitions, is never right on Flaw. We aren\'t ever complaining in the answers that we don\'t have precise data. We\'re complaining about the reasoning.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt doesn\'t matter what the specific profit amount (net income amount) is. If I tell you that Company X sold 1/2 as many hamburgers as last year, and that their net income per burger is the same, then we can validly derive that they made 1/2 as much net income from burgers.\\r\\n-- C: The conclusion is only about \\"the product\\", only about hair dryers. Information about other products has no bearing on how much net income they made specifically from hairdryers.\\r\\n-- D: Would it hurt the argument if we said that the price of Wilson\'s hair dryer increased? Couldn\'t we then argue that, \\"even though they\'re selling fewer hair dryers, they\'re still making around the same amount of money because they raised the price on those hair dryers\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nNo, we can\'t, because we have a factual premise that net income per hair dryer has stayed the same.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf it used to be sold for $20, but it cost $15 to make each one, then the company had $5 of net income on the sale of each hair dryer.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf over the past 5 years the price as been raised to $25, but the company still has $5 of net income on the sale of each hair dryer, that means that costs have gone up to $20.\\r\\n\\r\\nThere\'s no way to use more revenue as a way to mess with the author\'s math, because the author has already established that net income, which balances revenue and costs, is the same.\\r\\n-- E: When an answer starts off like presumes / takes for granted / fails to establish / provides no evidence that, it\'s trying to name something the argument needed to state but didn\'t. In other words, it\'s trying to name a necessary assumption of the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\nDid this argument need to assume that Wilson\'s hair dryer is one of the company\'s least profitable products?\\r\\n\\r\\nNot at all. It doesn\'t make a difference how the profitability of hair dryers compares to that of any other of Wilson\'s products. All we care about is whether the profitability of hair dryers changed at all since 5 years ago, but there\'s a premise telling us directly that it did not.\\r\\n===== PT120-S3-Q8 Flaw key A\\r\\nSTIM: Consumer advocate: There is ample evidence that the model of car one drives greatly affects the chances that one’s car will be stolen. The model of car stolen most often in our country last year, for example, was also the model stolen most often in the preceding year.\\r\\nSTEM: The consumer advocate\'s reasoning is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that it\\r\\n## Conclusion (ample evidence that)\\r\\nThe model of car one drives greatly affects the chances that one\'s car will be stolen.\\r\\n## Evidence\\r\\nThe model of car stolen most often in our country last year, for example, was also the model stolen most often in the preceding year.\\r\\n## Evaluation\\r\\nSince the author\'s evidence is a statistic and her conclusion is assuming a causal explanation for that statistic (\\"affects\\" = causal), we can use our Explain Curious Fact framework.\\r\\nCurious Fact ... why is the car that was stolen the most two years ago the same model as the car that was stolen the most last year?\\r\\nAuthor\'s Explanation ... it must be that this model is super desirable. Thieves apparently really want this model and seek it out. So when people choose to drive this model of car they\'re making it more likely that their car will be stolen.\\r\\nWe want always consider 2 things: - could there be some Alternate Explanation for the curious fact? - how plausible is the Author\'s Explanation?\\r\\nHowever, on Flaw questions, more than 90% of the time the correct answer cares about the author\'s failure to consider Alternate Explanations (we call this the **Causal** flaw, and it\'s one of the 10 famous flaws).\\r\\nSo what could be a different way to explain why the same model of car has won the title of \\"stolen most often\\" two years in a row? Is it really that thieves desire that specific model?\\r\\nMaybe it\'s just that this model happens to be really easy to break into; maybe it\'s an economy model and the people who can only afford to buy this modest car cannot afford to invest in other anti-theft devices. Thus it\'s not the model of car affecting their chance of the car being stolen, it\'s the fact that the people who buy this model don\'t put any anti-theft devices onto the car.\\r\\nOr maybe it\'s just the most common car, period. In America, there was a long stretch of years where the Honda Civic was the #1 most frequently bought car. All other things being equal, that would lead to the Honda Civic being the model of car most often stolen. It\'s not because thieves are seeking out that model. It\'s because they find Civics everywhere they look.\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nLook for an answer saying that the author is overconfidently assuming one potential explanation for the Curious Fact, failing to consider alternative explanations for why the same model of car has been \\"most stolen\\" two years running.\\r\\n-- A: Whenever a Flaw answer choice begins with fails to consider / overlooks the possibility, we can treat the idea that follows as though we\'re doing a Weaken question.\\r\\n\\r\\nWould it hurt the argument if we said, \\"Hey, author -- that model of car that\'s been #1 for car thefts the past two years is just the **most common** model of car\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nYes, because that would provide an Alternate Explanation for the evidence. The author thinks that the Civic is getting the \\"most stolen\\" trophy because thieves specifically want that model of car. But this answer is saying, \\"No, thieves are just stealing what\'s available.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nIf we all have a 1% chance of having our car stolen, regardless of what model we drive, then which ever model of car is most common will be the model that is most often stolen.\\r\\n\\r\\nThere might be 1000 Teslas and 100,000 Civics, and if in both cases 1% of owners will have their cars stolen, then 10 Teslas and 1,000 Civics will be stolen.\\r\\n-- B: Whenever a Flaw answer choice begins with fails to consider / overlooks the possibility, we can treat the idea that follows as though we\'re doing a Weaken question.\\r\\n\\r\\nWould it hurt the argument if we said, \\"Hey, author -- the age of a car **also** affects its chances of being stolen\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nNo, because the author never said that \\"model type is **the only / the main** factor that affects likelihood of being stolen\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nIf I say, \\"smoking two packs of cigarettes a day greatly affects your chances of getting cancer\\", you can\'t counter that by saying, \\"Other things also affect your chances of getting cancer\\".\\r\\n-- C: Whenever a Flaw answer choice begins with fails to consider / overlooks the possibility, we can treat the idea that follows as though we\'re doing a Weaken question.\\r\\n\\r\\nWould it hurt the argument if we said, \\"Hey, author -- that model of car that\'s been stolen most-often for the past tow years is stolen because it has a very high resale value\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nAt first that might feel like an Alternate Explanation, but since the resale value of a car is very related to the model type, this could actually still conform to the author\'s causal story.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you choose to drive a model of car (like say, a Toyota Prius) that has a high resale value, then you\'re providing car thieves with a higher incentive for stealing your car. Thus, the model of car you drive **is** affecting the chances that your car gets stolen.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen we compare this \\"alternative explanation\\" to that in (A), this one makes it seem like \\"there\'s something special about that model of car that makes it more attractive to thieves\\". In (A), it was just a mathematical explanation. There wasn\'t something special about the Civic that made it more a more attractive model to steal. There are just way more of them on the road, so even if every car is equally likely to be stolen, the Civic is the model of car that would be stolen most often.\\r\\n-- D: Whenever a Flaw answer choice begins with presumes / takes for granted, we can treat the idea that follows as though we\'re doing a Necessary Assumption question.\\r\\n\\r\\nDid this argument need to assume that, \\"the **only** thing thieves consider is model type\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nNo, that\'s way too strong. The author is definitely assuming that \\"one of the things that thieves consider is model type\\", but it doesn\'t need to be the only thing.\\r\\n-- E: Whenever a Flaw answer choice begins with presumes / takes for granted, we can treat the idea that follows as though we\'re doing a Necessary Assumption question.\\r\\n\\r\\nDid this argument need to assume that, \\"likelihood of getting your car stolen **should override** other considerations in deciding which car to buy\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nNo, the author isn\'t offering any advice about which car to buy, so she isn\'t making any assumptions about what we should / shouldn\'t do.\\r\\n"}'}]

Some members are at 300,000.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evaluate**\\r\\n  *Given that ...* hundreds of Sturdimades have made it past 100,000 miles,\\r\\n  *How could we argue that ...* you still can\'t rely on the Sturdimade *you* buy going the distance?\\r\\n\\r\\nLook at the entry requirement for this club. They\'re not in the club. They can\'t be. If Sturdimade has sold ten million cars, a few hundred survivors tells us very little about the odds for yours.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhenever an argument relies on a sample, we ask whether it\'s Too Small, Unrepresentative / Biased, or Self-Selecting. This one is biased by design.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Goal**\\r\\nLet\'s look for an answer describing the Famous Sampling flaw: the author generalizes to all Sturdimades from a group that was chosen precisely because it already has the trait the conclusion wants to prove. A conversational version would sound like \\"fails to consider how many Sturdimades didn\'t last long enough to join the club.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n### Choice Notes\\r\\n\\r\\n**(A) Correct.**\\r\\nThis is the Sampling flaw, described abstractly. Translate it back:\\r\\n- \\"draws a general conclusion\\": any Sturdimade you buy will last.\\r\\n- \\"from cases selected only on the basis of having a characteristic that favors that conclusion\\": club members, who got in only by already having driven 100,000+ miles.\\r\\n\\r\\nIs it descriptively accurate? Yes. Is it a problem with getting from the evidence to the conclusion? Yes. If the sample is filtered by the very trait you\'re trying to prove, the sample can\'t prove it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**(B) Wrong Flaw.**\\r\\nThis describes the famous Circular Reasoning flaw, where the evidence just restates the conclusion. The `Modelled on` references are complete explanations in that database, selected for the reasoning pattern. Tags follow the pilot's choice-note vocabulary. No question bank or database edits are part of this draft.\n+\n+## PT109-S3-Q5 | Flaw | D\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT150-S2-Q4 (comparison), PT141-S4-Q3 (exposure and baseline), PT139-S4-Q18 (quantity distinction).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** Radiation absorbed by airline flight crews does negligible harm.\n+\n+**Evidence:** Radiation absorbed on *one ordinary commercial flight* is no more dangerous than radiation from *one ordinary dental X-ray*, and one dental X-ray does negligible harm.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** The comparison changes its unit. A passenger takes one flight; a crew member takes many. Even if one exposure is harmless, repeated exposures might add up to harmful levels. Ask the author to compare cumulative exposure over the same period, not one flight against one X-ray.\n+\n+**Goal:** Find the objection that dose depends on duration and frequency, which the one-flight comparison omits.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Scope.** Other kinds of dangerous radiation do not challenge the conclusion about the radiation crews actually absorb. This would help only if crews were exposed to one of those other kinds.\n+\n+**(B) Irrelevant Difference.** A dental X-ray might offset some other risk, but the evidence says the X-ray itself does negligible harm. The conclusion concerns radiation harm, not total health benefits of dentistry versus flying.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Exposure.** Higher-intensity X-rays may be harmful. The argument compares an ordinary dental X-ray to an ordinary flight; it does not claim that every X-ray exposure is harmless.\n+\n+**(D) Correct.** The more often and longer someone is exposed, the more radiation that person can absorb. Crew members' repeated flights could cause nonnegligible harm even if the radiation from a single flight is as harmless as that from a single dental X-ray. This is the missing difference in the comparison.\n+\n+**(E) Wrong Risk.** Additional risks of flying may matter to airline crews' overall health, but the conclusion is limited to harm from radiation. An objection needs to make the radiation conclusion doubtful.\n+\n+## PT110-S3-Q4 | Flaw | E\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT147-S1-Q6 (unrepresentative sample), PT120-S3-Q8 (rate versus cause), PT109-S4-Q1 (sampling).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** The overwhelming majority of high school students want to finish, so a high *national* dropout rate could not be due to students' lack of desire.\n+\n+**Evidence:** More than 89 percent of 1,000 randomly selected students at three schools in one medium-sized city said they planned to finish.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** Random selection can make a sample representative of the population *from which it was drawn*. It does not make three schools in one city representative of the nation. Imagine that other regions have very different dropout pressures or aspirations. Also, a plan to finish is not proof that a student will finish, though the answer set focuses on the first gap.\n+\n+**Goal:** Look for an objection to projecting a local survey result to students nationwide.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Flaw.** Eighty-nine percent is plainly a large majority. Quibbling over the word \"overwhelming\" would not expose the national sampling problem.\n+\n+**(B) False Conflict.** Wanting to finish and dropping out can coexist. The two conclusions concern students' desire and a possible explanation of a dropout rate, not logically conflicting outcomes.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Target.** The argument says *if* the national dropout rate is high, lack of desire cannot explain it. It does not need a high rate actually to exist to make that conditional claim.\n+\n+**(D) False Contradiction.** Students can want to finish and still leave school. The author does not contradict himself by discussing both possibilities.\n+\n+**(E) Correct.** The respondents are from three schools in one medium-sized city, while the conclusion covers high school students nationwide. Even a large, randomly selected sample can be unrepresentative when drawn from the wrong population.\n+\n+## PT111-S3-Q13 | Flaw | D\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT148-S1-Q2 (rate and total), PT120-S4-Q23 (timeframe), PT151-S4-Q23 (track the conclusion's exact claim).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** The Planning Board exaggerates when it estimates that businesses are leaving at about four a week.\n+\n+**Evidence:** The region has never had more than about 1,000 businesses; at four departures per week, all would have been gone long ago.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** The Board's estimate could describe the *current* departure rate. The president multiplies that rate across an unspecified long past. Even with hardly any arrivals in ten years, departures might have been slower before and increased recently. For instance, a rate of four this week says nothing about how many left each week five years ago.\n+\n+**Goal:** Find the time shift from a present rate to a sustained historical rate.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Backwards.** The speaker explicitly notes that almost no new businesses have arrived. The mistake is extending the departure rate backwards, not ignoring incoming businesses.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Distinction.** A rate of four departures per week and a stock of at most 1,000 businesses are different quantities, but comparing them *would* be informative if the rate had lasted long enough. The unsupported move is assuming that it did.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Flaw.** The president does not attack the Board's interests or motives. He attacks its estimate with a numerical argument.\n+\n+**(D) Correct.** \"Leaving ... at the rate of about four a week\" reports a rate now. The president calculates as though businesses had been leaving at that rate for an extended time. That historical premise was never supplied.\n+\n+**(E) Wrong Complaint.** The president calls the estimate a gross exaggeration, not merely imprecise. His reasoning does not depend on pretending that \"about four\" is exact.\n+\n+## PT111-S3-Q25 | Flaw | D\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT134-S3-Q6 (causal assumption), PT128-S3-Q14 (correlation language), PT120-S3-Q8 (one factor versus only factor).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** Testosterone tends to promote heart disease, and estrogen tends to inhibit it.\n+\n+**Evidence:** Men develop heart disease earlier than women on average. Younger men have much more testosterone than younger women, and women lose estrogen after menopause, around the age when their heart disease tends to appear.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** The patterns line up, but alignment alone does not show that either hormone causes the disease pattern. Other age- or sex-related factors could produce both. We would need evidence that varying these hormone levels changes heart disease risk while relevant alternatives are controlled.\n+\n+**Goal:** Find the causal leap from correlation between hormone levels and disease timing to hormone effects.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Too Strong.** The physician need not believe hormones are the *primary* explanation. The conclusion says testosterone and estrogen *tend* to influence heart disease, which allows other factors to be important or even dominant.\n+\n+**(B) Too Strong.** Nothing here requires these to be the only relevant hormones. Another hormone could also promote or inhibit disease.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Comparison.** The speaker compares men and women at different life stages to suggest effects of hormones. The argument does not need young high-testosterone men to have higher risk than postmenopausal women.\n+\n+**(D) Correct.** The observed hormone differences and heart disease patterns are correlations. To conclude that testosterone promotes and estrogen inhibits the disease, the physician assumes those correlations reflect causal influence. A shared cause or another sex-related difference could instead explain the pattern.\n+\n+**(E) Too Strong.** Hormone levels could vary substantially within age and gender groups while the described average patterns still hold. The causal inference does not depend on identical levels among all people in a group.\n+\n+## PT111-S4-Q8 | Flaw | E\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT141-S4-Q3 (equal outcomes mask unequal baselines), PT147-S1-Q6 (measure does not establish broad conclusion), PT134-S3-Q6 (alternate explanation).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** The belief that perception and memory are significantly reduced by age 80 is false.\n+\n+**Evidence:** Eighty-year-olds and thirty-year-olds performed equally well on a card game devised to test those abilities.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** Does the game discriminate between high and moderately reduced ability? Suppose a score of 100 is perfect, and anyone with at least 40 units of perception and memory gets 100. A thirty-year-old with 90 and an eighty-year-old with 50 would tie despite a large decline. Equal game performance does not establish equal underlying ability.\n+\n+**Goal:** Look for a floor or ceiling problem: the game may be too easy to expose the alleged decline.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Ability.** Whether the game tests *other* cognitive abilities does not help assess whether it can detect differences in perception and memory.\n+\n+**(B) Opposite Direction.** If card games are among the hardest cognitive tasks, equal performance could be stronger evidence against the alleged decline. The author needs the game to be sensitive enough; this answer makes that more plausible.\n+\n+**(C) Irrelevant Relationship.** Perception and memory may interact, but the alleged flaw concerns whether the game can reveal a reduction in either. Interaction alone does not explain the tied scores despite a decline.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Source.** The origin of the belief, even prejudice, would not by itself show that the belief is false or that the study's game can detect an actual decline.\n+\n+**(E) Correct.** If perfect play takes only fairly low levels of the two abilities, both age groups could play perfectly even though eighty-year-olds' levels are significantly lower. A test with a low ceiling cannot rule out the decline the argument denies.\n+\n+## PT112-S3-Q25 | Flaw | D\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT154-S2-Q12 (false choice), PT134-S3-Q6 (alternative causal stories), PT120-S3-Q8 (one factor versus only factor).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** Diatonic music's popularity can be attributed *only* to innate dispositions of the human mind.\n+\n+**Evidence:** If social conditioning caused a scale's popularity, the anthropologist expects varied social systems to yield a varied mixture of scales. Instead, diatonic scales dominate much of the world's music.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** Even if a purely social explanation were inadequate, why must innate disposition act alone? Similar human dispositions could make diatonic music appealing, while social learning reinforces and spreads it. Ruling out one proposed explanation as complete does not rule out a combined explanation.\n+\n+**Goal:** Find the unjustified either-or: innate influence and social conditioning can both contribute.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Compatible.** Some people could prefer nondiatonic music while diatonic scales still dominate most music. Individual exceptions do not address whether social conditioning contributes to the broad pattern.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Scale.** The conclusion explains diatonic music's popularity, not how innate dispositions affect appreciation of nondiatonic music.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Burden.** A theory of why diatonic scales are popular need not explain the existence of every scale. The argument's issue is the exclusivity of the proposed cause.\n+\n+**(D) Correct.** Innate dispositions and social conditioning might jointly influence popularity. The failure of the anthropologist's prediction for a *purely* social explanation does not show that social conditioning contributes nothing.\n+\n+**(E) Outside Population.** The argument concerns patterns in human music and the human mind. Animal appreciation of nondiatonic music would not settle whether social conditioning helps explain human preference for diatonic scales.\n+\n+## PT112-S4-Q8 | Flaw | E\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT154-S1-Q12 (rebuttal structure), PT155-S1-Q20 (conclusion stronger than reply), PT134-S3-Q6 (alternate evidence).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** There was *no* early historical link between Old- and New-World civilizations.\n+\n+**Evidence:** Egyptian and Mayan pyramids differ in design and function; Egyptian ones were tombs, Mayan ones temples.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** Those differences may weaken one proposed piece of evidence for a link. But proving that these particular buildings were used differently would not settle whether some other evidence establishes contact. Nor does a historical link require identical building designs or uses. The conclusion \"no such link\" reaches well past the evidence about pyramids.\n+\n+**Goal:** Look for the move from challenging one line of evidence to treating the whole historical question as settled.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Flaw.** \"Evidence\" is used consistently as support for a historical link. The archaeologist disputes how strong the pyramid comparison is; no second meaning of the word drives the inference.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Flaw.** The argument cites design and function, not an emotional reaction intended to replace evidence.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Flaw.** The premise is about differences between pyramids; it does not simply restate the claim that there was no historical link.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Flaw.** The terms are sufficiently clear for the argument's purpose. The problem remains even if every feature and use is defined precisely.\n+\n+**(E) Correct.** Differences between the pyramids at most challenge one proposed indication of contact. The categorical conclusion assumes there is no other relevant evidence of an early link. That assumption is unsupported.\n+\n+## PT113-S4-Q16 | Flaw | C\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT147-S1-Q6 (population mismatch), PT150-S2-Q4 (salient difference), PT134-S3-Q6 (overlooked alternative).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** A connoisseur's assessment of a painting's authenticity cannot be given credence.\n+\n+**Evidence:** The emotional impact of art differs wildly from person to person, while connoisseurs say that emotional impact guides their assessments.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** The premise ranges over people generally. The conclusion concerns trained connoisseurs. Perhaps connoisseurs share criteria and agree with one another even when the broader public reacts differently. Variation across everyone does not establish unreliable judgments within the expert group.\n+\n+**Goal:** Find the gap between public variation and agreement among connoisseurs.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) No Objection.** Anyone may report an emotional response. The question is whether trained connoisseurs' responses can inform authenticity; public access to the same kind of response does not show they cannot.\n+\n+**(B) Misreads Example.** Rembrandt is introduced as an example of the claimed method, not as evidence that only his work has variable impact. The central premise is about artworks' impact across people.\n+\n+**(C) Correct.** Even if people generally react very differently to art, connoisseurs might agree widely about the impact relevant to attribution. If so, public variation would not justify dismissing the connoisseurs' assessments.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Assumption.** The speaker does not say emotional impact is irrelevant to authenticity. The complaint is that responses vary, which allegedly makes the proposed method unreliable. Those are different claims.\n+\n+**(E) Unsupported Topic.** The example contrasts a Rembrandt with a student's work, but the argument never compares Rembrandt's emotional skill with other painters'. Its reasoning turns on variation among observers.\n+\n+## PT114-S1-Q6 | Flaw | E\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT134-S3-Q6 (alternative explanation), PT155-S1-Q20 (rebuttal overreach), PT120-S3-Q8 (one aspect versus whole claim).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** Experts are useless for guiding health decisions.\n+\n+**Evidence:** One article called coffee dangerous, while another said coffee has some health benefits.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** Are the articles actually incompatible? A substance can harm health in one respect and help in another. Both experts could be right and useful: their advice may identify different effects that a decision maker should weigh. Even a genuine disagreement about coffee would be thin support for declaring all experts useless.\n+\n+**Goal:** Find the reconciliation of coffee's harms and benefits that dissolves the supposed contradiction.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Opposite.** The speaker presents one article as saying coffee is dangerous and another as saying it has benefits. He does not adopt the first article's conclusion as a premise.\n+\n+**(B) No Need.** The argument says expert guidance is useless, whether or not people always want such guidance. A desire for advice is not the logical bridge at issue.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Field.** The conclusion is about health decisions, so expert opinion in other areas cannot repair or expose the coffee-based inference.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Direction.** The author is skeptical of experts. He does not infer that all expert opinions are trustworthy because one is.\n+\n+**(E) Correct.** Coffee could be harmful in some respects and beneficial in others. The articles would then report different true facts, not contradictory advice. The argument's starting point for dismissing experts would fail.\n+\n+## PT114-S1-Q8 | Flaw | E\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT154-S1-Q12 (conflicting behavior ad hominem), PT155-S1-Q20 (source versus merits), PT150-S2-Q4 (plan objection).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** Town residents have no reason to follow officials' recommendation to use the airline more often.\n+\n+**Evidence:** Those officials drove to a conference last week instead of flying.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** An official can give sound advice while failing to follow it on one occasion. The conference trip might even have had special constraints, but we need not explain it: their conduct does not tell us whether more ticket sales would help keep service. Test the recommendation on its merits.\n+\n+**Goal:** Look for the assumption that a speaker's failure to practice advice makes the advice worthless.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Speaker's Burden.** More ticket sales might or might not make the route viable. The pundit does not assume that they will; the town officials' recommendation suggests that possibility. The pundit's flaw is dismissing their advice by pointing to their behavior.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Issue.** The airline may have options besides suspension and continued losses. The pundit's inference about whether residents should follow the recommendation does not rely on a two-option view of the airline's choices.\n+\n+**(C) Irrelevant Funding.** Who paid for the officials' trip does not determine whether encouraging residents to fly is sound advice.\n+\n+**(D) Irrelevant Cost Pattern.** A general comparison of ground and air transport prices would not establish that the officials' advice should be ignored. The route's profitability is the relevant issue.\n+\n+**(E) Correct.** The pundit uses the officials' failure to follow their own recommendation as the reason to reject the recommendation. That is a version of the ad hominem mistake: hypocrisy can be real while the advice remains sound.\n+\n*** End Patch";
text(await tools.apply_patch(patch));
