[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 10.5 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"c7f9ca","wall_time_seconds":10.011227,"session_id":89875,"original_token_count":5377,"output":"Warning: truncated output (original token count: 5377)\\nTotal output lines: 221\\n\\n===== PT119-S2-Q24 Flaw key D\\r\\nSTIM: It is now clear that the ancient Egyptians were the first society to produce alcoholic beverages. It had been thought that the ancient Babylonians were the first; they had mastered the process of fermentation for making wine as early as 1500 B.C. However, archaeologists have discovered an Egyptian cup dating from 2000 B.C. whose sides depict what appears to be an Egyptian brewery, and whose chemical residue reveals that it contained a form of alcoholic beer.\\r\\nSTEM: The reasoning above is most vulnerable to criticism on which one of the following grounds?\\r\\n## Conclusion\\r\\nThe ancient Egyptians were the first society to produce alcoholic beverages.\\r\\n## Evidence\\r\\nAn Egyptian cup dating from 2000 B.C. depicts a brewery and contains residue of alcoholic beer — older than the Babylonian fermentation evidence from 1500 B.C.\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\n\\"First known\\" and \\"first ever\\" are different. The Egyptian cup is the earliest *archaeological evidence* of alcoholic beverage production we have found, but other societies — including the Egyptians themselves earlier or some entirely different civilization — could have produced alcohol earlier without leaving evidence behind, or with evidence we have not yet found.\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nFind an answer that points out the gap between \\"earliest evidence we have\\" and \\"first ever.\\"\\r\\n===== PT113-S2-Q3 Flaw key B\\r\\nSTIM: Joan got A’s on all her homework assignments, so if she had gotten an A on her term paper, she could pass the course even without doing the class presentation. Unfortunately, she did not get an A on her term paper, so it is obvious that she will have to do the class presentation to pass the course.\\r\\nSTEM: The argument\'s reasoning is questionable because the argument\\r\\n## Conclusion (so it is obvious that)\\r\\nJoan will have to do the class presentation to pass the course.\\r\\n## Evidence\\r\\nIf Joan had gotten an A on her term paper, she could pass the course without doing the class presentation.\\r\\nBut she didn\'t get an A on her term paper.\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nWhenever we\'re doing Flaw and we see any Conditional Logic, we should immediately be suspicious that the argument is going to commit an illegal negation or reversal (aka, the #1 Famous Flaw, **Necessary vs. Sufficient** ).\\r\\nThe first sentence has a conditional rule, signified by **if**.\\r\\nIf A on term paper, then pass without presentation.\\r\\nSo an illegal reversal would sound like, \\"Joan passed the course without doing the class presentation. Thus, she must have gotten an A on her term paper.\\"\\r\\nAn illegal negation would sound like, \\"Joan didn\'t get an A on her term paper, so she can\'t pass without first doing the presentation.\\"\\r\\nAnd that second thing is exactly what the argument did!\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nWe can describe this flaw formally as confusing Necessary and Sufficient conditions. We were told in the evidence that getting an A on her term paper would have been **sufficient** to get Joan out of having to do the class presentation.\\r\\nBut the author is acting like it\'s **necessary**. He\'s acting like if she didn\'t get the A then she must do the class presentation.\\r\\nWe can describe the flaw conversationally by saying, \\"Fails to consider that there could be other things Joan potentially could have done to pass the course without doing the class presentation\\" (a doctor\'s note / extra credit / a bribe or threat from her parents / etc.)\\r\\n-- A: This author **takes for granted** / **presumes** that Joan has to either get an A on her term paper or do the class presentation. So this answer would be correct if it began with any of the assumption prefixes.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt doesn\'t **fail to consider / ignore the possibility** that she has to get an A or do the class presentation.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe former are prefixes that indicate what the author was thinking or assuming. The latter are prefixes that raise a potential objection to the argument. When we see fails to consider / overlooks the possibility that X, we ask ourselves if X would weaken.\\r\\n\\r\\nWould it hurt the argument if we said, \\"Hey, author -- Joan has to either get an A on her term paper or do the class presentation to pass the course\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nDefinitely not. That completely matches the author\'s thinking.\\r\\n-- B: Since this answer starts with takes for granted / presumes / presupposes that X, we can just ask ourselves, \\"Was the author assuming X?\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nDid this author assume that Joan\'s not getting an A on the term paper prevents her from passing the course without doing the class presentation?\\r\\n\\r\\nYes! That perfectly matches the reasoning move. The author establishes that Joan didn\'t get an A, and then moves from that idea to the conclusion that she will have to do the class presentation in order to pass the course.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf we negated this assumption (not getting an A **doesn\'t** prevent Joan from passing without doing the class presentation), would that weaken?\\r\\n\\r\\nTotally! This author thinks she has to do the class presentation in order to pass, but this negation would be saying the opposite.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhy is it bad for the author to be assuming that not getting an A prevents Joan from passing the course without doing the class presentation?\\r\\n\\r\\nBecause we were never told that passing the course w/o doing the class presentation was dependent on getting an A.\\r\\n\\r\\nWe know that getting an A is **one potential way** that you can pass the course without doing the class presentation (sufficient). But we were never told it\'s the **only way** to pass the course without doing the class presentation (necessary), as the author seems to be thinking.\\r\\n-- C: Nothing in the argument has anything to do with her overall course grade, so this feels out of scope.\\r\\n\\r\\nSince the answer begins with fails to consider / overlooks the possibility that X, we want to ask ourselves if X could weaken.\\r\\n\\r\\nHere, X isn\'t expressed as a clause but rather as a long phrase. Still we could turn it into a clause.\\r\\n\\r\\nWe can ask ourselves, \\"Would it hurt the author\'s argument if we said Hey, author -- class presentations are very important to a student\'s overall grade\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nNo, that wouldn\'t hurt the argument at all. The author was never talking about overall grade nor was she assuming anything about whether class presentations have or don\'t have an important connection to overall grade.\\r\\n-- D: Like (A), this answer is pairing up something the author thinks with the prefix fails to consider / ignores the possibility.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe author\'s premise says, If get A on term paper, then don\'t need to do CP\\r\\n\\r\\nby contrapositive that means, if do need to do CP, then didn\'t get A on term paper\\r\\n\\r\\nThose two things mean the same thing. A conditional and its contrapositive have the same meaning, just like \\"X is bigger than Y\\" has the same meaning as \\"Y is smaller than X\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nSo the author\'s premise said \\"X > Y\\" and this answer is saying the author ignored the possibility that \\"Y < X\\". The author announced that possibility; she didn\'t ignore it.\\r\\n-- E: Since this answer starts with fails to consider / ignores the possibility that X, we can just ask ourselves, \\"Does X weaken?\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nCan we object to this argument by saying, \\"Yo, author -- some students get A\'s on their term paper but don\'t pass the course\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nNope. The author\'s argument is about someone who **didn\'t** get an A on their term paper, so whatever is true of people who **do** get an A on their term paper is irrelevant to this conversation about Joan\'s situation.\\r\\n===== PT154-S2-Q12 Flaw key E\\r\\nSTIM: Bauer: It is a mistake to criticize the city for being overzealous in its issuance of parking tickets. Can you imagine how much worse parking would be if parking regulations were not enforced?\\r\\nSTEM: The reasoning in Bauer\'s argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that the argument\\r\\n## Conclusion\\r\\nIt is a mistake to criticize the city for being overzealous in its issuance of parking tickets.\\r\\ntranslation:\\r\\nThe city\'s not being overzealous (or if they are, we shouldn\'t criticize them for it).\\r\\n## Evidence\\r\\nCan you imagine how much worse parking would be if parking regulations were not enforced?\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nThis is a Straw Man argument, which is a term people use in real life to describe it, but LSAT has never used that wording to describe it. The idea of a Straw Man rebuttal is that you\'re unfairly representing the position being debated.\\r\\nIf I say, \\"I think we should extend unemployment benefits another six months, while we recover from the pandemic\\" and you say, \\"Well I don\'t think it\'s the government\'s job to make people rich for doing nothing!\\" ...\\r\\n... you\'re not arguing with my position; you\'re fighting a Straw Man position you designed, because it makes for an easy opponent (a piñata you can knock down).\\r\\nExtending unemployment benefits (which means paying a jobless person around $1200 / month, which is well below the poverty line) is not making people rich for doing nothing. That\'s not an intellectually respectful rebuttal to the original position.\\r\\nIn this parking ticket argument, the original position in the critics\':\\r\\nthe city is being overzealous in its issuance of parking tickets.\\r\\nThink about what that means. Give yourself a mental picture, a couple examples. What does it mean for a meter maid to be issuing tickets overzealously?\\r\\nMaybe the meter runs out for some car at 5:58pm, but the rules for that spot are that you only need to pay until 6pm, after which it\'s free. Rather than letting it slide, the overzealous meter maid gives you a ticket for those 2 unpaid minutes.\\r\\nMaybe your car is parked a little too far up, in terms of the parking spot lines or in terms of the red part of the curb where you can\'t park. Rather than tolerate your front bumper being six inches over the red line, they overzealously give you a ticket for illegal parking.\\r\\nThe author\'s method of arguing is saying, \\"You shouldn\'t criticize the status quo, because the alternative is worse!\\" You shouldn\'t criticize this obnoxiously overzealous ticketing by the meter maids, because the alternative, a wold in…377 tokens truncated…quence is a must be true implication of an idea. When we write conditionals, such as A → B, we say that B is a consequence of A. The practice of enforcing parking tickets does not logically guarantee overzealous enforcement. So we can\'t say overzealousness is a logical consequence.\\r\\n\\r\\nA causal consequence is a result that follows from this action transpiring. A causal consequence of enforcing parking tickets would be things like\\r\\n- the city gets more revenue\\r\\n- the driver of that car gets furious and the rest of their day is crappy\\r\\n\\r\\nOverzealous parking enforcement isn\'t a consequence of the practice of enforcing parking. It is just one style of conducting that practice.\\r\\n-- B: If a flaw answer takes the form\\r\\ntakes for granted that X merely because Y\\r\\nwe\'ll ask ourselves whether X matches the conclusion and Y matches the support (because).\\r\\n\\r\\nDid the author conclude that a certain authority should be respected?\\r\\n\\r\\nEh. We could make it work. She concluded that it\'s wrong to criticize meter maids for being overzealous, so that\'s in the neighborhood of \\"respect their authority\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nDoes the premise say something like, \\"after all, all authorities should be respected\\"? No, not at all. So we definitely can\'t match up the merely because it is an authority part.\\r\\n-- C: If a flaw answer takes the form\\r\\ntakes for granted that X simply because Y\\r\\nwe\'ll ask ourselves whether X matches the conclusion and Y matches the support (because).\\r\\n\\r\\nDid the author conclude that \\"a particular practice is good\\"? No, she concluded that it\'s wrong to criticize meter maids for being overzealous. That\'s not close enough to keep reading.\\r\\n\\r\\nAs it turns out, the premise part is miles away. The evidence never mentions \\"how things have been traditionally done\\".\\r\\n-- D: This describes one response to the famous Causal flaw. This argument had no causal interpretations happening. The author wasn\'t trying to explain what was causing overzealous ticketing. She was just defending its existence.\\r\\n\\r\\nNote: this formulation of the Causal flaw answer is always wrong, because the answer is sure of itself. We can never definitively say that the author confused cause for effect. We can only point out the possibility that she failed to consider that possibility.\\r\\n-- E: If a flaw answer takes the form\\r\\ndoes X merely by doing Y\\r\\nwe\'ll ask ourselves whether X matches the conclusion and Y matches the support (by).\\r\\n\\r\\nWas the author\'s conclusion defending the current situation?\\r\\n\\r\\nYes. it was saying, \\"Back off these meter maids, y\'all. Stop criticizing them for overzealous ticketing.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nWas the author\'s evidence saying that current situation is superior to some alternative scenario?\\r\\n\\r\\nYes, the author is arguing that things would be worse (inferior) if parking regulations were not enforced at all.\\r\\n\\r\\nIs that alternative scenario implausible?\\r\\n\\r\\nYes! That\'s a ridiculous Straw Man position. Who is saying \'Defund the Parking Police\'? We\'re just saying, \\"please stop being a jerk about giving tickets when we\'re just a few inches over the red line or when there\'s like a 2 minute unpaid gap between when the meter runs out and when the spot becomes a free spot.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nWhy would any city go to the trouble of creating parking regulations, posting signs, installing meters, etc., and then do zero enforcement? No one is suggesting that.\\r\\n===== PT155-S1-Q20 Flaw key B\\r\\nSTIM: Mr. Klemke argues that the complaints recently lodged against his roofing company are unfounded, on the grounds that each of the complainants disagrees with Klemke\'s widely known political views and is therefore biased. However, having a different political outlook from Klemke\'s would in no way prevent one from being badly treated by his company. Clearly, then, the complaints are not, as Mr. Klemke alleges, unfounded.\\r\\nSTEM: The argument against Mr. Klemke\'s allegation is flawed in that it\\r\\n## Conclusion (Clearly, then)\\r\\nThe complaints are not unfounded.\\r\\n  rephrase: There is legit reason to believe the complaints.\\r\\n## Evidence\\r\\nKlemke says that the complainants disagree with his widely known political views and they are therefore biased.\\r\\n  But having different politics is still compatible with being badly treated by his company (different politics in no way prevents you from being badly treated).\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nWhenever we\'re doing Flaw and we see a Rebuttal style argument, we want to look for the two Top 10 Famous Flaws that deal with rebuttals:\\r\\n  1. Ad Hominem (dismissing someone\'s view because the source is biased or because the source has conflicting past behavior)\\r\\n  2. Unproven vs. Proven False (explaining why one person\'s attempt to prove X fails, but then illicitly concluding that this shows that X is false)\\r\\n  #1 is actually what Klemke is doing. He\'s committing an Ad Hominem flaw by dismissing the views of the complainants simply because they are a biased source (they don\'t like his politics).\\r\\n  Our author points out to Klemke that this is a flawed argument. You can\'t ignore complaints about your roofing company just because the source of the complaints is biased. That\'s a flawed / inadequate argument.\\r\\n  But #2 does seem to be what the author is doing. The easiest way to spot Unproven vs. Proven False is to notice that the author\'s conclusion is the complete opposite of the opponent\'s conclusion.\\r\\n  For example:\\r\\n  Sheila thinks 5G networks cause cancer, but the research paper she found to support that judgment turns out to be forged by a conspiracy theorist. Therefore, 5G networks do not cause cancer.\\r\\n  The correct, responsible conclusion to draw when someone makes an unsuccessful argument in favor of X is that, \\"We don\'t currently know whether X is true.\\" After all, how does this author know that 5G networks don\'t cause cancer? Does he have evidence to support that claim?\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nIn this argument, Klemke was alleging that \\"these complaints are unfounded\\". The author explains to us why Klemke\'s support for that allegation was flawed and inadequate.\\r\\n  But the author isn\'t allowed to go to the opposite side of the spectrum and say, \\"Clearly, then the complaints are well founded\\".\\r\\n  You can\'t say, \\"The committee didn\'t find criminal wrongdoing in Patrick\'s dealings with Russia. Therefore, Patrick had no criminal wrongdoing in his dealings with Russia.\\"\\r\\n  Let\'s look for something describing the illegal Unproven vs. Proven False move, or pointing out that even though Klemke\'s reason for rejecting the complaints was a bogus one, there still might be a legitimate reason to reject the complaints.\\r\\n-- A: This answer is saying that our author took the consequence/effect of something to be the cause of something.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe nickname for that concept is Reverse Causality. An author might see a correlation between X and Y and conclude that X causes Y, while failing to consider that Y really caused X. In such an argument, we could say that the author \\"took a consequence of Y to be a cause of Y\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nBut nothing in this argument has anything to do with causality, so this does not match anything.\\r\\n-- B: This describes the famous Unproven vs. Proven False flaw.\\r\\n\\r\\nOn earlier LSAT\'s, this flaw was easier to spot:\\r\\n\\"No one has ever proven that cell phones cause cancer. Thus, cell phones don\'t cause cancer\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nAnd the answer would say \\"Confuses an absence of evidence with evidence of absence\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nBut on more modern tests, instead of saying \\"there\'s a lack of evidence for X\\", they like to do, \\"Someone made a crappy argument in favor of X\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nTheir argument for X rested on a dubious assumption, or in this case made some illegal Ad Hominem move.\\r\\n\\r\\nBut just because their reasoning was too faulty to prove X doesn\'t mean we have any grounds for assuming X is false.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf we weren\'t familiar with this Top 10 famous flaw, then we would simply look at the form of this answer, Concludes X on the grounds that Y, and try to match X to the conclusion and Y to the evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\nDid the author conclude that a claim is false?\\r\\n\\r\\nYes, \\"Mr. Klemke\'s allegation that the complaints are unfounded is false.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nWas the evidence talking about an inadequate argument being offered for the claim that the complaints are unfounded?\\r\\n\\r\\nYes, it told us about how Klemke unsuccessfully tried to prove that the complaints were unfounded by pointing to political bias on the part of the complainants.\\r\\n\\r\\nSince everything matches, and since it describes an objectionable move, we can pick it.\\r\\n-- C: This answer describes the top 10 famous flaw, Ad Hominem, in which an author dismisses a point of view because the source is biased or has conflicting past behavior.\\r\\n\\r\\nOur author is trying to dismiss Klemke\'s view. Does she do so by citing a premise that \\"Klemke is biased?\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nNo. Klemke committed this flaw in how he reasoned about the complainants. Our author didn\'t commit this flaw.\\r\\n-- D: This refers to the top 10 famous flaw Sampling, in which an argument relies on a sample of data points that we have reason to believe is too small, self-selecting, unrepresentative, biased, or otherwise problematic.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis argument doesn\'t rely on a sample at all. Our author attempts to prove that Klemke\'s allegation is wrong by discussing Klemke\'s flawed way of dismissing the complaints.\\r\\n-- E: When a Flaw answer choice starts with fails to consider / overlooks the possibility, we can ask ourselves whether the idea that follows would potentially weaken the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\nCould we say, \\"Hey, author — the complaints are unfounded. After all, people whose views diverge are unaware of their disagreement.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nThat doesn\'t feel like it makes any sense.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe complainants have view that widely diverge from Klemke\'s political views. Do we know or care whether the complainants are aware of that disagreement?\\r\\n\\r\\nWe don\'t know and we don\'t care. That has more to do with Klemke\'s argument, whereas we\'re here to critique this author who is saying, \\"Since Klemke\'s reason for dismissing the complaints as unfounded was a dumb reason, the complaints must be well founded.\\"\\r\\n"}'}]

## Tool: exec

const patch="*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/corpus/lr-explanations/drafts/batch-01-flaw-part-b.md\n@@\n **(E) Correct.** The pundit uses the officials' failure to follow their own recommendation as the reason to reject the recommendation. That is a version of the ad hominem mistake: hypocrisy can be real while the advice remains sound.\n+\n+## PT114-S2-Q25 | Flaw | B\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT147-S1-Q6 (unrepresentative sample), PT148-S1-Q2 (proportion versus total), PT120-S3-Q8 (base rate).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** The universe contains an extremely large number of planets fit to sustain some form of life.\n+\n+**Evidence:** One of the nine planets in our solar system qualifies, and the universe has astonishingly many planetary systems.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** Multiplying one solar system's proportion by many systems assumes that our system resembles a great many others in the relevant respect. If most other systems contain no life-sustaining planets, a huge number of systems would not validate the projected ratio. The argument offers one observed system as a sample of the universe.\n+\n+**Goal:** Find the assumption that our solar system is sufficiently typical of other planetary systems.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Outcome.** A planet being fit to sustain life does not guarantee that life arises on it. The conclusion concerns *fitness*, not the actual origin of life.\n+\n+**(B) Correct.** The argument extrapolates from our system's one-in-nine ratio to numerous other systems. That inference needs many other systems to be similar to ours in the prevalence of life-sustaining planets. Without that, the local ratio tells us little about the universe-wide count.\n+\n+**(C) Unneeded Knowledge.** We need not understand how life begins to assess the premise's stipulated judgment that Earth is fit to sustain it. The projection across systems is the gap.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Claim.** The argument does not require Earthlike life to evolve elsewhere, or require life to evolve at all. It counts planets capable of sustaining *some form* of life.\n+\n+**(E) Too Exact.** Other systems need not have exactly nine planets. They need a comparable frequency of life-sustaining planets for the extrapolation to work. Nine is the denominator in our system, not a required size for every system.\n+\n+## PT114-S4-Q8 | Flaw | D\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT154-S1-Q12 (hypocrisy and merits), PT155-S1-Q20 (evaluate the rebuttal), PT113-S2-Q3 (prefix test).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** The opponent's argument for changing zoning laws should not be taken seriously.\n+\n+**Evidence:** Although the opponent advocates encouraging housing such as apartment buildings, he himself lives in a house in the country.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** Living in a country house does not settle whether current zoning rules overpromote suburban single-family homes. The opponent could even prefer his own home while correctly arguing that the city should make room for other residents' choices. The politician needs to answer the housing-policy claim, not the opponent's lifestyle.\n+\n+**Goal:** Identify the ad hominem move: using a person's conduct to dismiss the merits of that person's argument.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Unsupported Motive.** The politician may be prejudiced against apartments, but describing the opponent's residence does not establish that motive. The flaw we can verify is reliance on an irrelevant personal fact.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Distinction.** Where apartment buildings can be built does not address whether the opponent's home undermines his argument. His proposal can be assessed without this suburban-versus-center comparison.\n+\n+**(C) Irrelevant Comparison.** Knowing the politician's own living arrangement would not make the opponent's argument more or less sound.\n+\n+**(D) Correct.** Whether the opponent lives in a country house has no bearing on whether zoning laws should encourage more varied housing. The criticism attacks the advocate's lifestyle while leaving the policy reasoning unanswered.\n+\n+**(E) Irrelevant History.** The opponent need never have lived in an apartment for his present claim about zoning to be sound. A past apartment residence would not be the missing defense of his argument.\n+\n+## PT115-S2-Q14 | Flaw | C\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT154-S2-Q12 (false choice), PT150-S2-Q4 (plan/recommendation), PT120-S3-Q8 (one factor not only factor).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** Legislators should vote against the environmental bill.\n+\n+**Evidence:** It is popular but would have mainly negative economic consequences, especially for attracting new businesses. Great leaders look beyond popularity to sound policy.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** The bill's economic costs matter, and popularity by itself would not justify it. But a vote requires weighing *all* important consequences. Environmental or health benefits might outweigh those costs. The speaker gives a reason against the bill and treats it as decisive without examining potential reasons for it.\n+\n+**Goal:** Find the omitted noneconomic benefits that could outweigh the acknowledged economic downside.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Unneeded Identity.** Legislators need not be great leaders for the activist to urge them to follow the conduct attributed to great leaders.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Outcome.** The conclusion recommends how legislators ought to vote, not a prediction of whether the bill is likely to pass.\n+\n+**(C) Correct.** The economic drawback may be outweighed by noneconomic reasons to support the bill. Without comparing the costs with potential benefits, the activist cannot conclude that sound policy requires voting no.\n+\n+**(D) Irrelevant Habit.** Whether legislators *usually* consider economics does not determine how they ought to vote on this bill. The gap lies in the omitted reasons for passing it.\n+\n+**(E) Opposite.** The activist explicitly grants that the bill is popular while arguing that it is economically harmful. He does not infer harm from popularity.\n+\n+## PT115-S4-Q21 | Flaw | B\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT147-S1-Q6 (sampling), PT120-S3-Q8 (base-rate alternative), PT148-S1-Q2 (relative versus absolute).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** Moore is a poor plumber who cannot be counted on to do good work.\n+\n+**Evidence:** In one small town, every filed complaint about a plumber's work was against Moore, despite Moore's relatively small business.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** That sounds damning until we ask for the comparison group. Are there many other plumbers receiving no complaints, or does Moore do nearly all the plumbing in town? If Moore is the only plumber, every plumbing complaint would necessarily name Moore, even if his work is generally good. The distribution of complaints alone does not establish his reliability.\n+\n+**Goal:** Look for the missing base rate: how many other plumbers could have received complaints?\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Measurement.** Customers without complaints might matter if we knew how many there were, but their *interests* do not explain why Moore received all filed complaints.\n+\n+**(B) Correct.** There may be few or no competing plumbers. If Moore performs almost all the plumbing work, all complaints naming him would be unsurprising and would not establish that his work is unusually poor. The argument needs a meaningful comparison.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Size Test.** A small business can still produce enough jobs to support a judgment. The specific gap is the number of other plumbers and jobs available for comparison, not a bare question about whether Moore's business is \"too small.\"\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Trade.** Complaints about electricians, carpenters, or other tradespeople would not tell us whether Moore performs plumbing work well.\n+\n+**(E) Wrong Direction.** If more dissatisfied Moore customers failed to complain, that would make the known complaints undercount dissatisfaction and could strengthen the criticism. It does not expose why *all filed plumbing complaints* point to Moore.\n+\n+## PT116-S2-Q5 | Flaw | D\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT113-S2-Q3 (necessary versus sufficient), PT151-S4-Q23 (conditional translation), PT154-S1-Q9 (contrapositive).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion (customer):** She must pay for air express to receive the shipment tomorrow.\n+\n+**Evidence she attributes to the coordinator:** Without air express, it will *not* arrive tomorrow.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** The coordinator guaranteed tomorrow morning for air express. For ground carrier, the coordinator offered *tomorrow or the next day*. The customer turns \"ground may take until the next day\" into \"ground cannot arrive tomorrow.\" A guarantee of tomorrow by one method does not mean that method is required for tomorrow delivery.\n+\n+**Goal:** Find the conditional the customer falsely hears: no air express means no tomorrow delivery.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Property.** Reliability of ground shipping does not explain the customer's belief that it cannot arrive tomorrow.\n+\n+**(B) Actual Statement.** The coordinator did say air express would arrive tomorrow. That guarantee alone does not rule out ground carrier also arriving tomorrow.\n+\n+**(C) Actual Cost Relation.** Ground carrier is less expensive. That fact explains why the question is a choice, but not why the customer thinks ground cannot meet tomorrow's deadline.\n+\n+**(D) Correct.** \"Unless air express, not tomorrow\" means that air express is necessary for tomorrow delivery. That is exactly the customer's mistaken interpretation. The coordinator said ground might arrive tomorrow, so the supposed necessity is false.\n+\n+**(E) Wrong Generalization.** The customer need not believe every more expensive shipment is faster. Her mistake concerns the specific alternatives and the certainty of next-day arrival.\n+\n+## PT117-S3-Q4 | Flaw | A\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT113-S2-Q3 (necessary condition mistaken), PT150-S2-Q4 (comparison across contexts), PT134-S3-Q6 (alternative route).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion (Frank):** Today, one needs a formal education to make crucial technological contributions.\n+\n+**Evidence:** Since Edison's day, technology has developed so much that today's contributors need far more extensive technical knowledge than he did.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** Frank may be right about the need for *knowledge*. But he has switched from knowledge to *formal education*. Someone could acquire deep technical knowledge through independent study, apprenticeship, work, or other routes. Edison having little schooling does not prove that today's knowledge demands can be met without schooling, but Frank's reply likewise fails to establish that schooling is required.\n+\n+**Goal:** Find the distinction between needing technical knowledge and needing formal education.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Correct.** Technical knowledge could be acquired without formal schooling. If so, even a much higher knowledge requirement today would not refute Maria's claim that formal education is unnecessary. Frank treats one route to knowledge as the only route.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Comparison.** Improvements in schools since Edison's day might make formal education more useful, but Frank's conclusion says it is *necessary*. School quality does not close the gap between knowledge and credentials.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Term.** Both speakers use \"crucial contributions\" to mean consequential technological work. Their dispute is over what preparation that work requires today.\n+\n+**(D) Irrelevant Peer.** Frank does not need to assume Edison was uniquely productive. His response concerns today's knowledge requirements, regardless of other inventors in Edison's era.\n+\n+**(E) No Need to Dispute Facts.** Frank can grant everything Maria says about Edison and still argue that today's conditions differ. His flaw lies in the inference about formal education, not in failing to challenge the historical example.\n+\n+## PT117-S4-Q9 | Flaw | D\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT134-S3-Q6 (alternate explanation), PT150-S2-Q4 (comparison difference), PT120-S3-Q8 (one factor versus only factor).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** Sullivan's older age was not the deciding factor in his failed promotion.\n+\n+**Evidence:** Several recently promoted people were older than Sullivan.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** The relevant question is whether age disadvantaged Sullivan *relative to his competitors for that job*. Other promotion contests may have had different age comparisons. An older person could win a contest against still older applicants while Sullivan loses against younger ones. The general record of older promotees does not settle what happened in Sullivan's particular contest.\n+\n+**Goal:** Look for a difference in the other promotion contests: the older promotees may not have faced younger competitors.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Insufficient.** Being well qualified does not show that age was the deciding factor; Sullivan might have lost to an even better qualified candidate.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Role.** That age was *one* factor keeping Sullivan from promotion is consistent with it being the deciding factor. The number of other factors does not rebut the manager's argument.\n+\n+**(C) Background Generality.** Some people associate age with good judgment, but that does not show how age worked in Sullivan's promotion decision or in the cited promotions.\n+\n+**(D) Correct.** If the older promotees had no younger competitors, their promotions do not show that an older applicant would beat a younger one. Sullivan could still have lost because he was much older than the competition in his particular contest.\n+\n+**(E) Irrelevant Secrecy.** Confidential deliberations might make the true reason hard to know, but secrecy does not explain why the manager's evidence fails to rule age out.\n+\n+## PT118-S3-Q6 | Flaw | C\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT154-S1-Q12 (source attack), PT155-S1-Q20 (rebuttal overreach), PT114-S1-Q8 (advice versus advisor).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion:** The premier should reject the advisor's recommendation and abandon hope of cutting taxes without a significant service reduction.\n+\n+**Evidence:** The advisor was convicted of embezzlement in his youth, so his economic advice is allegedly untrustworthy.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** A past conviction may justify scrutinizing the person, but it says little about the specific economic prediction. Wasteful spending might be large enough to cut without reducing services. The editorial offers no calculation or policy evidence to test that claim. Rejecting the source leaves the substance unresolved.\n+\n+**Goal:** Identify the attack on the claimant in place of an examination of the claim.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Rejection.** The editorial does not analyze a particular implementation of spending cuts and predict its failure. It dismisses the proposal by invoking the advisor's past.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Direction.** The editorial is not promoting an alternative proposal by stoking fear of its rejection. It is arguing against the advisor's proposal.\n+\n+**(C) Correct.** The source's youthful embezzlement conviction is used to discredit the present economic claim. Whether wasteful spending can be eliminated sufficiently requires evidence about budgets and services, which the editorial does not examine.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Flaw.** The writer does not merely say there is no evidence for the advisor's claim. He offers a personal reason to distrust the advisor, then treats that as grounds to reject the claim.\n+\n+**(E) Wrong Flaw.** The premise about a conviction does not restate the conclusion about tax cuts and services. The reasoning is flawed, but it is not circular.\n+\n+## PT119-S2-Q24 | Flaw | D\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT155-S1-Q20 (absence of proof versus proof), PT112-S4-Q8 (one piece of evidence does not settle history), PT148-S1-Q2 (word-level distinction).\n+\n+The existing database explanation already has Conclusion, Evidence, Evaluate, and Goal steps. The notes below complete that explanation; the existing steps should be retained.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Sample.** The author is not using one Egyptian cup to generalize about what most Egyptians did. The cup establishes an instance of alcoholic beer in Egypt by 2000 B.C.; the issue is whether that instance was the world's first.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Flaw.** \"Alcoholic beverage\" includes wine and beer in both the evidence and the conclusion. The argument compares dates, not two meanings of the term.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Claim.** The author does not say Babylonian wine production depended on Egyptian beer production. Being earlier than one known Babylonian example is offered as evidence for priority, not for transmission of technology.\n+\n+**(D) Correct.** The cup is older than the cited Babylonian evidence, so Egypt has the earlier *known* example of the two. Another society might have made alcohol even earlier without leaving evidence we have found. The oldest discovered instance need not be the first historical instance.\n+\n+**(E) Wrong Target.** The author supplies the 1500 B.C. Babylonian wine date as background. Even if that date were well documented, the Egyptian cup would not establish that no society produced alcohol before Egypt.\n+\n+## PT119-S3-Q2 | Flaw | C\n+\n+**Modelled on:** PT154-S2-Q12 (answer the current proposal), PT155-S1-Q20 (weak rebuttal does not settle opposing claim), PT150-S2-Q4 (conditions can differ across times).\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion (Willett):** He opposes the new zoning regulations restricting growth.\n+\n+**Evidence:** Similar arguments for restricting growth arose ten and five years ago, and the council was justified in rejecting them then. Willett says there is nothing new in the idea.\n+\n+**Evaluate:** A familiar *proposal* can have new reasons behind it. Growth might have changed the city's housing, congestion, or infrastructure since the last decision. Even if those earlier councils were right, their decisions do not automatically settle today's case. Willett must assess the present evidence for Benson's quality-of-life claim.\n+\n+**Goal:** Find the possibility that new circumstances or reasons arose after the earlier decisions.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Too Strong.** Willett need not think all growth is good. He can oppose this particular restriction while accepting that some growth has costs. His stated reason is the earlier rejections.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Rebuttal.** Willett does not attack Benson's character. He attacks the age of the proposal and cites past council decisions.\n+\n+**(C) Correct.** The idea of restricting growth can be old while reasons supporting it are new. Changes in the past five years could make today's regulation warranted even though earlier councils rightly rejected earlier restrictions.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Complaint.** Many factors may affect quality of life, but Willett does not attempt a full quality-of-life analysis. The flaw in *his* argument is using the oldness of the proposal as if it ruled out new justification.\n+\n+**(E) Unneeded Attack.** The council ten years ago may have been well qualified and correct in its own circumstances. That still would not decide whether present reasons support new zoning rules.\n*** End Patch";
text(await tools.apply_patch(patch));

const cmd=`@'
import builtins, io, json, re, runpy
original_open = builtins.open
gaps = [x for x in json.load(original_open('corpus/lr-explanations/work/pilot-inputs/gaps.json', encoding='utf-8')) if x['type'] == 'Flaw'][20:40]
picks = []
for x in gaps:
    m = re.fullmatch(r'PT(\\d+)-S(\\d+)-Q(\\d+)', x['slot'])
    pt, sec, num = map(int, m.groups())
    records = json.load(original_open(f'projects/lsatu-wt-glossary/public/data/PT{pt}-S{sec}-LR.json', encoding='utf-8'))
    b = next(v for v in records if v['questionNumber'] == num)
    picks.append(dict(slot=x['slot'], fam='Flaw', diff=b.get('questionDifficulty'), feat=b.get('questionFeatures') or [], stimulus=b['stimulus'], stem=b['questionStem']))
def memory_open(path, mode='r', *args, **kwargs):
    if path == 'pilot/picks.json':
        return io.StringIO(json.dumps(picks))
    if path in ('pilot/cand.json', 'pilot/neighbors.json') and 'w' in mode:
        return io.StringIO()
    return original_open(path, mode, *args, **kwargs)
builtins.open = memory_open
runpy.run_path('system/scripts/lr-explanations/neighbors.py', run_name='__main__')
'@ | python -`;
const r=await tools.exec_command({cmd,login:false,yield_time_ms:10000,max_output_tokens:5500});text(r);

g=[x for x in json.load(open('corpus/lr-explanations/work/pilot-inputs/gaps.json',encoding='utf-8')) if x['type']=='Flaw'][20:40]
q={f"PT{v['preptest']}-S{v['section']}-Q{v['question_number']}":v for v in json.load(open('system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/lr_questions.json',encoding='utf-8'))}
ac={}
for c in json.load(open('system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/lr_answer_choices.json',encoding='utf-8')):ac[(c['question_id'],c['letter'])]=c
for x in g:
 m=re.fullmatch(r'PT(\\d+)-S(\\d+)-Q(\\d+)',x['slot']);pt,sec,num=map(int,m.groups())
 b=next(v for v in json.load(open(f'projects/lsatu-wt-glossary/public/data/PT{pt}-S{sec}-LR.json',encoding='utf-8')) if v['questionNumber']==num)
 d=q[x['slot']]
 mismatches=[]
 if b['correctAnswer']!=d['correct_choice']:mismatches.append('KEY')
 if b['stimulus'].strip()!=d['stimulus'].strip():mismatches.append('STIM')
 if b['questionStem'].strip()!=d['prompt'].strip():mismatches.append('STEM')
 for letter,t in b['answerChoices'].items():
  z=ac.get((d['id'],letter))
  if z is None or t.strip()!=z['choice_text'].strip():mismatches.append(letter)
 print(x['slot'],'mismatches='+','.join(mismatches) if mismatches else 'EXACT','tags='+','.join(f"{k}:{ac[(d['id'],k)]['tag'] or '-'}:{ac[(d['id'],k)]['student_percent']}" for k in 'ABCDE'))

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 1.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"e0348b","wall_time_seconds":0.8297798,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":5037,"output":"**(A) Wrong Scope.** Other kinds of dangerous radiation do not challenge the conclusion about the radiation crews actually absorb. This would help only if crews were exposed to one of those other kinds.\\n**(B) Irrelevant Difference.** A dental X-ray might offset some other risk, but the evidence says the X-ray itself does negligible harm. The conclusion concerns radiation harm, not total health benefits of dentistry versus flying.\\n**(C) Wrong Exposure.** Higher-intensity X-rays may be harmful. The argument compares an ordinary dental X-ray to an ordinary flight; it does not claim that every X-ray exposure is harmless.\\n**(D) Correct.** The more often and longer someone is exposed, the more radiation that person can absorb. Crew members\' repeated flights could cause nonnegligible harm even if the radiation from a single flight is as harmless as that from a single dental X-ray. This is the missing difference in the comparison.\\n**(E) Wrong Risk.** Additional risks of flying may matter to airline crews\' overall health, but the conclusion is limited to harm from radiation. An objection needs to make the radiation conclusion doubtful.\\n**(A) Wrong Flaw.** Eighty-nine percent is plainly a large majority. Quibbling over the word \\"overwhelming\\" would not expose the national sampling problem.\\n**(B) False Conflict.** Wanting to finish and dropping out can coexist. The two conclusions concern students\' desire and a possible explanation of a dropout rate, not logically conflicting outcomes.\\n**(C) Wrong Target.** The argument says *if* the national dropout rate is high, lack of desire cannot explain it. It does not need a high rate actually to exist to make that conditional claim.\\n**(D) False Contradiction.** Students can want to finish and still leave school. The author does not contradict himself by discussing both possibilities.\\n**(E) Correct.** The respondents are from three schools in one medium-sized city, while the conclusion covers high school students nationwide. Even a large, randomly selected sample can be unrepresentative when drawn from the wrong population.\\n**(A) Backwards.** The speaker explicitly notes that almost no new businesses have arrived. The mistake is extending the departure rate backwards, not ignoring incoming businesses.\\n**(B) Wrong Distinction.** A rate of four departures per week and a stock of at most 1,000 businesses are different quantities, but comparing them *would* be informative if the rate had lasted long enough. The unsupported move is assuming that it did.\\n**(C) Wrong Flaw.** The president does not attack the Board\'s interests or motives. He attacks its estimate with a numerical argument.\\n**(D) Correct.** \\"Leaving ... at the rate of about four a week\\" reports a rate now. The president calculates as though businesses had been leaving at that rate for an extended time. That historical premise was never supplied.\\n**(E) Wrong Complaint.** The president calls the estimate a gross exaggeration, not merely imprecise. His reasoning does not depend on pretending that \\"about four\\" is exact.\\n**(A) Too Strong.** The physician need not believe hormones are the *primary* explanation. The conclusion says testosterone and estrogen *tend* to influence heart disease, which allows other factors to be important or even dominant.\\n**(B) Too Strong.** Nothing here requires these to be the only relevant hormones. Another hormone could also promote or inhibit disease.\\n**(C) Wrong Comparison.** The speaker compares men and women at different life stages to suggest effects of hormones. The argument does not need young high-testosterone men to have higher risk than postmenopausal women.\\n**(D) Correct.** The observed hormone differences and heart disease patterns are correlations. To conclude that testosterone promotes and estrogen inhibits the disease, the physician assumes those correlations reflect causal influence. A shared cause or another sex-related difference could instead explain the pattern.\\n**(E) Too Strong.** Hormone levels could vary substantially within age and gender groups while the described average patterns still hold. The causal inference does not depend on identical levels among all people in a group.\\n**(A) Wrong Ability.** Whether the game tests *other* cognitive abilities does not help assess whether it can detect differences in perception and memory.\\n**(B) Opposite Direction.** If card games are among the hardest cognitive tasks, equal performance could be stronger evidence against the alleged decline. The author needs the game to be sensitive enough; this answer makes that more plausible.\\n**(C) Irrelevant Relationship.** Perception and memory may interact, but the alleged flaw concerns whether the game can reveal a reduction in either. Interaction alone does not explain the tied scores despite a decline.\\n**(D) Wrong Source.** The origin of the belief, even prejudice, would not by itself show that the belief is false or that the study\'s game can detect an actual decline.\\n**(E) Correct.** If perfect play takes only fairly low levels of the two abilities, both age groups could play perfectly even though eighty-year-olds\' levels are significantly lower. A test with a low ceiling cannot rule out the decline the argument denies.\\n**(A) Compatible.** Some people could prefer nondiatonic music while diatonic scales still dominate most music. Individual exceptions do not address whether social conditioning contributes to the broad pattern.\\n**(B) Wrong Scale.** The conclusion explains diatonic music\'s popularity, not how innate dispositions affect appreciation of nondiatonic music.\\n**(C) Wrong Burden.** A theory of why diatonic scales are popular need not explain the existence of every scale. The argument\'s issue is the exclusivity of the proposed cause.\\n**(D) Correct.** Innate dispositions and social conditioning might jointly influence popularity. The failure of the anthropologist\'s prediction for a *purely* social explanation does not show that social conditioning contributes nothing.\\n**(E) Outside Population.** The argument concerns patterns in human music and the human mind. Animal appreciation of nondiatonic music would not settle whether social conditioning helps explain human preference for diatonic scales.\\n**(A) Wrong Flaw.** \\"Evidence\\" is used consistently as support for a historical link. The archaeologist disputes how strong the pyramid comparison is; no second meaning of the word drives the inference.\\n**(B) Wrong Flaw.** The argument cites design and function, not an emotional reaction intended to replace evidence.\\n**(C) Wrong Flaw.** The premise is about differences between pyramids; it does not simply restate the claim that there was no historical link.\\n**(D) Wrong Flaw.** The terms are sufficiently clear for the argument\'s purpose. The problem remains even if every feature and use is defined precisely.\\n**(E) Correct.** Differences between the pyramids at most challenge one proposed indication of contact. The categorical conclusion assumes there is no other relevant evidence of an early link. That assumption is unsupported.\\n**(A) No Objection.** Anyone may report an emotional response. The question is whether trained connoisseurs\' responses can inform authenticity; public access to the same kind of response does not show they cannot.\\n**(B) Misreads Example.** Rembrandt is introduced as an example of the claimed method, not as evidence that only his work has variable impact. The central premise is about artworks\' impact across people.\\n**(C) Correct.** Even if people generally react very differently to art, connoisseurs might agree widely about the impact relevant to attribution. If so, public variation would not justify dismissing the connoisseurs\' assessments.\\n**(D) Wrong Assumption.** The speaker does not say emotional impact is irrelevant to authenticity. The complaint is that responses vary, which allegedly makes the proposed method unreliable. Those are different claims.\\n**(E) Unsupported Topic.** The example contrasts a Rembrandt with a student\'s work, but the argument never compares Rembrandt\'s emotional skill with other painters\'. Its reasoning turns on variation among observers.\\n**(A) Opposite.** The speaker presents one article as saying coffee is dangerous and another as saying it has benefits. He does not adopt the first article\'s conclusion as a premise.\\n**(B) No Need.** The argument says expert guidance is useless, whether or not people always want such guidance. A desire for advice is not the logical bridge at issue.\\n**(C) Wrong Field.** The conclusion is about health decisions, so expert opinion in other areas cannot repair or expose the coffee-based inference.\\n**(D) Wrong Direction.** The author is skeptical of experts. He does not infer that all expert opinions are trustworthy because one is.\\n**(E) Correct.** Coffee could be harmful in some respects and beneficial in others. The articles would then report different true facts, not contradictory advice. The argument\'s starting point for dismissing experts would fail.\\n**(A) Wrong Speaker\'s Burden.** More ticket sales might or might not make the route viable. The pundit does not assume that they will; the town officials\' recommendation suggests that possibility. The pundit\'s flaw is dismissing their advice by pointing to their behavior.\\n**(B) Wrong Issue.** The airline may have options besides suspension and continued losses. The pundit\'s inference about whether residents should follow the recommendation does not rely on a two-option view of the airline\'s choices.\\n**(C) Irrelevant Funding.** Who paid for the officials\' trip does not determine whether encouraging residents to fly is sound advice.\\n**(D) Irrelevant Cost Pattern.** A general comparison of ground and air transport prices would not establish that the officials\' advice should be ignored. The route\'s profitability is the relevant issue.\\n**(E) Correct.** The pundit uses the officials\' failure to follow their own recommendation as the reason to reject the recommendation. That is a version of the ad hominem mistake: hypocrisy can be real while the advice remains sound.\\n**(A) Wrong Outcome.** A planet being fit to sustain life does not guarantee that life arises on it. The conclusion concerns *fitness*, not the actual origin of life.\\n**(B) Correct.** The argument extrapolates from our system\'s one-in-nine ratio to numerous other systems. That inference needs many other systems to be similar to ours in the prevalence of life-sustaining planets. Without that, the local ratio tells us little about the universe-wide count.\\n**(C) Unneeded Knowledge.** We need not understand how life begins to assess the premise\'s stipulated judgment that Earth is fit to sustain it. The projection across systems is the gap.\\n**(D) Wrong Claim.** The argument does not require Earthlike life to evolve elsewhere, or require life to evolve at all. It counts planets capable of sustaining *some form* of life.\\n**(E) Too Exact.** Other systems need not have exactly nine planets. They need a comparable frequency of life-sustaining planets for the extrapolation to work. Nine is the denominator in our system, not a required size for every system.\\n**(A) Unsupported Motive.** The politician may be prejudiced against apartments, but describing the opponent\'s residence does not establish that motive. The flaw we can verify is reliance on an irrelevant personal fact.\\n**(B) Wrong Distinction.** Where apartment buildings can be built does not address whether the opponent\'s home undermines his argument. His proposal can be assessed without this suburban-versus-center comparison.\\n**(C) Irrelevant Comparison.** Knowing the politician\'s own living arrangement would not make the opponent\'s argument more or less sound.\\n**(D) Correct.** Whether the opponent lives in a country house has no bearing on whether zoning laws should encourage more varied housing. The criticism attacks the advocate\'s lifestyle while leaving the policy reasoning unanswered.\\n**(E) Irrelevant History.** The opponent need never have lived in an apartment for his present claim about zoning to be sound. A past apartment residence would not be the missing defense of his argument.\\n**(A) Unneeded Identity.** Legislators need not be great leaders for the activist to urge them to follow the conduct attributed to great leaders.\\n**(B) Wrong Outcome.** The conclusion recommends how legislators ought to vote, not a prediction of whether the bill is likely to pass.\\n**(C) Correct.** The economic drawback may be outweighed by noneconomic reasons to support the bill. Without comparing the costs with potential benefits, the activist cannot conclude that sound policy requires voting no.\\n**(D) Irrelevant Habit.** Whether legislators *usually* consider economics does not determine how they ought to vote on this bill. The gap lies in the omitted reasons for passing it.\\n**(E) Opposite.** The activist explicitly grants that the bill is popular while arguing that it is economically harmful. He does not infer harm from popularity.\\n**(A) Wrong Measurement.** Customers without complaints might matter if we knew how many there were, but their *interests* do not explain why Moore received all filed complaints.\\n**(B) Correct.** There may be few or no competing plumbers. If Moore performs almost all the plumbing work, all complaints naming him would be unsurprising and would not establish that his work is unusually poor. The argument needs a meaningful comparison.\\n**(C) Wrong Size Test.** A small business can still produce enough jobs to support a judgment. The specific gap is the number of other plumbers and jobs available for comparison, not a bare question about whether Moore\'s business is \\"too small.\\"\\n**(D) Wrong Trade.** Complaints about electricians, carpenters, or other tradespeople would not tell us whether Moore performs plumbing work well.\\n**(E) Wrong Direction.** If more dissatisfied Moore customers failed to complain, that would make the known complaints undercount dissatisfaction and could strengthen the criticism. It does not expose why *all filed plumbing complaints* point to Moore.\\n**(A) Wrong Property.** Reliability of ground shipping does not explain the customer\'s belief that it cannot arrive tomorrow.\\n**(B) Actual Statement.** The coordinator did say air express would arrive tomorrow. That guarantee alone does not rule out ground carrier also arriving tomorrow.\\n**(C) Actual Cost Relation.** Ground carrier is less expensive. That fact explains why the question is a choice, but not why the customer thinks ground cannot meet tomorrow\'s deadline.\\n**(D) Correct.** \\"Unless air express, not tomorrow\\" means that air express is necessary for tomorrow delivery. That is exactly the customer\'s mistaken interpretation. The coordinator said ground might arrive tomorrow, so the supposed necessity is false.\\n**(E) Wrong Generalization.** The customer need not believe every more expensive shipment is faster. Her mistake concerns the specific alternatives and the certainty of next-day arrival.\\n**(A) Correct.** Technical knowledge could be acquired without formal schooling. If so, even a much higher knowledge requirement today would not refute Maria\'s claim that formal education is unnecessary. Frank treats one route to knowledge as the only route.\\n**(B) Wrong Comparison.** Improvements in schools since Edison\'s day might make formal education more useful, but Frank\'s conclusion says it is *necessary*. School quality does not close the gap between knowledge and credentials.\\n**(C) Wrong Term.** Both speakers use \\"crucial contributions\\" to mean consequential technological work. Their dispute is over what preparation that work requires today.\\n**(D) Irrelevant Peer.** Frank does not need to assume Edison was uniquely productive. His response concerns today\'s knowledge requirements, regardless of other inventors in Edison\'s era.\\n**(E) No Need to Dispute Facts.** Frank can grant everything Maria says about Edison and still argue that today\'s conditions differ. His flaw lies in the inference about formal education, not in failing to challenge the historical example.\\n**(A) Insufficient.** Being well qualified does not show that age was the deciding factor; Sullivan might have lost to an even better qualified candidate.\\n**(B) Wrong Role.** That age was *one* factor keeping Sullivan from promotion is consistent with it being the deciding factor. The number of other factors does not rebut the manager\'s argument.\\n**(C) Background Generality.** Some people associate age with good judgment, but that does not show how age worked in Sullivan\'s promotion decision or in the cited promotions.\\n**(D) Correct.** If the older promotees had no younger competitors, their promotions do not show that an older applicant would beat a younger one. Sullivan could still have lost because he was much older than the competition in his particular contest.\\n**(E) Irrelevant Secrecy.** Confidential deliberations might make the true reason hard to know, but secrecy does not explain why the manager\'s evidence fails to rule age out.\\n**(A) Wrong Rejection.** The editorial does not analyze a particular implementation of spending cuts and predict its failure. It dismisses the proposal by invoking the advisor\'s past.\\n**(B) Wrong Direction.** The editorial is not promoting an alternative proposal by stoking fear of its rejection. It is arguing against the advisor\'s proposal.\\n**(C) Correct.** The source\'s youthful embezzlement conviction is used to discredit the present economic claim. Whether wasteful spending can be eliminated sufficiently requires evidence about budgets and services, which the editorial does not examine.\\n**(D) Wrong Flaw.** The writer does not merely say there is no evidence for the advisor\'s claim. He offers a personal reason to distrust the advisor, then treats that as grounds to reject the claim.\\n**(E) Wrong Flaw.** The premise about a conviction does not restate the conclusion about tax cuts and services. The reasoning is flawed, but it is not circular.\\n**(A) Wrong Sample.** The author is not using one Egyptian cup to generalize about what most Egyptians did. The cup establishes an instance of alcoholic beer in Egypt by 2000 B.C.; the issue is whether that instance was the world\'s first.\\n**(B) Wrong Flaw.** \\"Alcoholic beverage\\" includes wine and beer in both the evidence and the conclusion. The argument compares dates, not two meanings of the term.\\n**(C) Wrong Claim.** The author does not say Babylonian wine production depended on Egyptian beer production. Being earlier than one known Babylonian example is offered as evidence for priority, not for transmission of technology.\\n**(D) Correct.** The cup is older than the cited Babylonian evidence, so Egypt has the earlier *known* example of the two. Another society might have made alcohol even earlier without leaving evidence we have found. The oldest discovered instance need not be the first historical instance.\\n**(E) Wrong Target.** The author supplies the 1500 B.C. Babylonian wine date as background. Even if that date were well documented, the Egyptian cup would not establish that no society produced alcohol before Egypt.\\n**(A) Too Strong.** Willett need not think all growth is good. He can oppose this particular restriction while accepting that some growth has costs. His stated reason is the earlier rejections.\\n**(B) Wrong Rebuttal.** Willett does not attack Benson\'s character. He attacks the age of the proposal and cites past council decisions.\\n**(C) Correct.** The idea of restricting growth can be old while reasons supporting it are new. Changes in the past five years could make today\'s regulation warranted even though earlier councils rightly rejected earlier restrictions.\\n**(D) Wrong Complaint.** Many factors may affect quality of life, but Willett does not attempt a full quality-of-life analysis. The flaw in *his* argument is using the oldness of the proposal as if it ruled out new justification.\\n**(E) Unneeded Attack.** The council ten years ago may have been well qualified and correct in its own circumstances. That still would not decide whether present reasons support new zoning rules.\\n"}'}]

Model references are from the database; weaknesses noted in `models/Flaw.md` were not copied.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 1. PT101-S3-Q2 · Flaw · Difficulty 1 · Answer E\\r\\n\\r\\n*Modelled on PT150-S2-Q4 (comparison), PT107-S4-Q18 (test the abstract wording), and PT154-S2-Q12 (missing alternative).*\\r\\n\\r\\n> Advertisement: Anyone who thinks moisturizers are not important for beautiful skin should consider what happens to the earth, the skin of the world, in times of drought. Without regular infusions of moisture the ground becomes lined and cracked and its lush loveliness fades away. Thus your skin, too, should be protected from the ravages caused by lack of moisture; give it the protection provided by regular infusions of Dewyfresh, the drought-defying moisturizer.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> The Dewyfresh advertisement exhibits which one of the following errors of reasoning?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> (A) It treats something that is necessary for bringing about a state of affairs as something that is sufficient to bring about that state of affairs.\\r\\n> (B) It treats the fact that two things regularly occur together as proof that there is a single thing that is the cause of them both.\\r\\n> (C) It overlooks the fact that changing what people think is the case does not necessarily change what is the case.\\r\\n> (D) It relies on the ambiguity of the term “infusion,” which can designate either a process or the product of that process.\\r\\n> (E) It relies on an analogy between two things that are insufficiently alike in the respects in which they would have to be alike for the conclusion to be supported.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Argument Breakdown\\r\\n\\r\\n**Conclusion (thus)** Skin should get regular Dewyfresh moisturizer to protect it from damage caused by lack of moisture.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidence** Drought dries and cracks the earth, which the advertisement calls “the skin of the world.”\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evaluate** Given that dry ground cracks, how could skin still behave differently? Soil and human skin are different materials. The ad needs the two to resemble each other in the very process it is comparing. The phrase “skin of the world” is a metaphor, not evidence that a moisturizer affects skin the way rain affects soil. Even if skin needs moisture, nothing here establishes that Dewyfresh provides the promised protection.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Goal** Look for a criticism of an analogy between the earth and human skin that has not established the relevant similarity.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Choice Notes\\r\\n\\r\\n**(A) Wrong Flaw.** Necessary versus sufficient would require the ad to argue, for example, that because moisture is required for healthy skin, applying Dewyfresh guarantees beautiful skin. It does not make that formal move. Its evidence is the earth analogy.\\r\\n\\r\\n**(B) Wrong Flaw.** A common cause would explain why two observed events happen together. The ad does not report two correlated events and infer a shared cause. It compares two different things.\\r\\n\\r\\n**(C) Wrong Flaw.** The argument does not claim that changing beliefs changes physical reality. The opening addresses people who doubt moisturizers, but the evidence is about drought, not a change in what people believe.\\r\\n\\r\\n**(D) Wrong Flaw.** “Infusion” is used to mean an input of moisture in both places. The conclusion does not switch from a process to its product. The questionable switch is from ground to human skin.\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

const cmd=`@'
import json,re
p='corpus/lr-explanations/drafts/batch-01-flaw-part-b.md'
s=open(p,encoding='utf-8').read()
head=re.compile(r'(?m)^(## (PT(\\d+)-S(\\d+)-Q(\\d+)) \\| Flaw \\| [A-E]\\n\\n\\*\\*Modelled on:\\*\\*[^\\n]+\\n)')
matches=list(head.finditer(s))
assert len(matches)==20,len(matches)
for m in reversed(matches):
 slot,pt,sec,num=m.group(2),int(m.group(3)),int(m.group(4)),int(m.group(5))
 bank=json.load(open(f'projects/lsatu-wt-glossary/public/data/PT{pt}-S{sec}-LR.json',encoding='utf-8'))
 b=next(v for v in bank if v['questionNumber']==num)
 def quote(t):return '\\n'.join('> '+line for line in t.splitlines())
 lines=[quote(b['stimulus']),'>',quote(b['questionStem']),'>']
 lines += [quote(f'({letter}) {b["answerChoices"][letter]}') for letter in 'ABCDE']
 block='\\n'+'\\n'.join(lines)+'\\n'
 s=s[:m.end()]+block+s[m.end():]
open(p,'w',encoding='utf-8',newline='\\n').write(s)
print('Inserted exact bank quotation blocks:',len(matches))
'@ | python -`;
